exhibit superluminal propagation around positive energy backgrounds
[
147
]
. While this behaviour is unusual, it is not immediately inconsistent, provided one stays in infinite volume.

T\bar{T}
and strings in uniform lightcone gauge

As it turns out, there exist also other ways of relating a
T\bar{T}
- deformed sigma model to strings. As nicely explained in
[
161
]
, it is possible to realise the
T\bar{T}
-deformed solution for the energies
E^{[\mu]}(R)=E^{[0]}(R+\mu E)
by studying strings in the so-called uniform lightcone gauge

X^{+}=\tau+a\sigma\;,\;\;\;\;\;p_{+}=1

(3.50)

where
a
is a constant,
X^{\pm}
are certain
a
- dependent linear combinations of the target time and space (both assumed to be isometry directions in the target space) and
p_{+}
is the lightcone momentum density
[
162
]
. Requiring that the target space energy not depend on the choice of gauge parameter
a
then implies the above relation between the energies. The radius of the
\sigma
coordinate in this gauge does, nonetheless, depend on the uniform lightcone gauge parameter, which is to be identified with the
T\bar{T}
coupling up to a numerical shift.

The above method for reproducing the spectrum of
T\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs is quite general, as it works for
\sigma
models involving scalars and fermions, with arbitrary potential. One interesting output of this way of understanding the
T\bar{T}
deformation is that it allowed writing a general formula for the deformed Hamiltonian density in terms of the undeformed one. The same formula has been obtained later in
[
163
]
by simply solving the
T\bar{T}
flow equation for the classical Hamiltonian density, as we now explain.

The general classical deformed Hamiltonian

The setup of
[
163
]
consisted of a classical two-dimensional field theory with classical fields
\phi_{k}
and conjugate momenta
\pi_{k}
, treated in the Hamiltonian formalism. They considered the
T\bar{T}
flow of the classical Hamiltonian density

\partial_{\mu}\mathcal{H}=-(T_{tt}T_{\sigma\sigma}-T_{t\sigma}^{2})

(3.51)

where the components of the stress tensor are obtained from the Hamiltonian density via

T_{tt}=\mathcal{H}\;,\;\;\;\;\;T_{t\sigma}=\mathcal{P}=\pi^{k}\phi^{\prime}_{k}\;,\;\;\;\;\;T_{\sigma t}=\partial_{\pi^{k}}\mathcal{H}\,\partial_{\phi^{\prime}_{k}}\mathcal{H}\;,\;\;\;\;\;T_{\sigma\sigma}=\pi^{k}\partial_{\pi^{k}}\mathcal{H}+\phi^{\prime}_{k}\partial_{\phi^{\prime}_{k}}\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{H}

(3.52)

with repeated indices summed over. If the trace of the stress tensor in the undeformed theory vanishes identically, then
\mathcal{H}^{[0]}
is a homogenous function of degree two in
\pi^{k},\phi^{\prime}_{k}
and the flow equation implies that the deformed Hamiltonian density is just a function of the undeformed one,
\mathcal{H}^{[0]}
and the momentum density,
\mathcal{P}
. Once this is established, the solution to (
3.51
) is trivial to find in closed form

\mathcal{H}^{[\mu]}(\pi^{k},\phi_{k})=\frac{1}{2\mu}\left(\sqrt{1+4\mu\mathcal{H}^{[0]}(\pi,\phi)+4\mu^{2}\mathcal{P}^{2}}-1\right)

(3.53)

Interestingly, the functional form of the relation is identical to the relation (
3.20
) between deformed and undeformed energies on the cylinder, though it does not follow from it in any simple way
17
17

17

It was already observed in
[
133
]
that the relation (
3.53
), guessed from the relation between the energies, satisfies the flow equation (
3.51
).
. It is also trivial to check that if one inputs the Hamiltonian for a free boson,
\mathcal{H}^{[0]}=\frac{1}{2}(\pi^{2}+\phi^{\prime 2})
, into this formula, one obtains the one that follows from the Lagrangian (
3.39
).

Given the fixed relation (
3.53
) between the deformed and undeformed Hamiltonian, it follows that the Poisson brackets of
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{P}
are
universal
- even though not particularly simple - since they follow from those of the undeformed CFT via this relation. This fact will be extremely important in section
5
, where we work out the classical symmetries of
T\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs.

Given the explicit form for
\mathcal{H}^{[\mu]}
in terms of
\mathcal{H}^{[0]}
, one may compute the
T_{\sigma\sigma}
component of the deformed stress tensor explicitly and use the result to show that classical
T\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs satisfy a trace relation

tr\,T=-2\mu\,\mathcal{O}_{T\bar{T}}=-\mu(T^{\alpha\beta}T_{\alpha\beta}-T^{2})

(3.54)

Thus, despite the fact that the
T\bar{T}
deformation breaks scale invariance and generates a trace for the stress tensor, the latter still only has two independent components off-shell, similarly to a
2d
CFT
[
149
]
.

Last but not least, let us point out that the general form of the solution (
3.53
) can also be obtained by assuming the deformed Hamiltonian is a function of just the undeformed one and the momentum, and requiring consistency of the equal-time Poisson brackets
[
164
]
. This entirely fixes the form of (
3.53
), up to a constant shift, and the
T\bar{T}
flow equation is obeyed automatically, upon correctly identifying the non-trivial arbitrary constant in the solution with
1/\mu
. It would be interesting if this analysis could be extended to seed field theories where the trace of the stress tensor does not vanish.

III. Scattering and gravitational dressing

Let us now turn back to the quantum theory. In integrable QFTs,
2\rightarrow 2
scattering is characterised by a phase, which is related to the ground state energy in finite size via the Thermodynamic Bethe Ansatz (TBA) equations
[
165
]
. Since the
T\bar{T}
deformation induces a universal change in the energy levels of the finite-size system and preserves integrability if initially present, one would expect the universal modification of the spectrum translates to a universal modification of the scattering phase. This is indeed the case, and turns out to be true beyond the realm of integrable QFTs.

To have a well-defined S-matrix in the first place, one should consider either a QFT containing massive particles, or the scattering of non-interacting massless ones
[
166
]
. Much of the early work on this subject
[
167
,
30
]
was performed for the latter case, where the problem of scattering
T\bar{T}
- deformed free bosons can be mapped to the scattering of worldsheet excitations
around a long string background. Despite the simplicity of the system, this work has gone a long way to clarify the physical meaning of the
T\bar{T}
scattering phase and how it reflects the non-locality of the underlying theory: its basic physical manifestations are a time delay in scattering proportional with the energy and the existence of a minimum length, both of which point towards a (quantum)-gravitational interpretation of
T\bar{T}
- deformed QFTs. The same works investigated whether the relation between
T\bar{T}
and strings survives at the quantum level, and paved the way for developing i) a non-perturbative definition of general
T\bar{T}
- deformed QFTs in terms of coupling the original QFT to topological gravity and ii) the relation between
T\bar{T}
and the effective theory of e.g. the QCD string. The latter research strand has not only proven to be a successful approach for matching lattice QCD data, but also shows that the study of
T\bar{T}
- like theories is relevant not just for abstract theoretical considerations, but also for the study of Nature.

In this subsection, we address these issues roughly in the order mentioned above.

Extracting the scattering phase from the
T\bar{T}
- deformed free boson spectrum

The free boson theory is a trivial example of an integrable theory - in particular, it has an infinite number of conserved charges, roughly given by
\int d\sigma(\partial\phi)^{n}
. Since, as we have seen above, the
T\bar{T}
-deformed free boson is related to the undeformed free boson by a simple change of gauge, the deformed theory is also integrable; else, one can use the general arguments of
[
27
]
to show integrability. This can be also seen explicitly from the exact finite-size spectrum (
3.21
) of
D-2

T\bar{T}
- deformed free bosons

E(N,\tilde{N},R)=\sqrt{\left(\frac{R}{2\mu}\right)^{2}+\frac{2\pi}{\mu}\left(N+\tilde{N}-\frac{D-2}{12}\right)+\left(\frac{2\pi(N-\tilde{N})}{R}\right)^{2}}-\frac{R}{2\mu}

(3.55)

where
N,\tilde{N}
are the levels for the left/right-movers
18
18

18

The same formula is obtained by quantizing a bosonic string in
D
target space dimensions in lightcone gauge, in the winding one sector
[
30
]
; the exact relation between the two systems will be discussed in the next subsubsection.
. Since states with a definite particle number are exact energy eigenstates, one concludes there should be no particle production in scattering.

Consider now the
2\rightarrow 2
scattering of these bosons, taken to be identical for simplicity. Before the
T\bar{T}
deformation, the S-matrix is simply one, and can be defined despite the absence of a mass gap because the particles do not interact. Since the deformed CFT is integrable, the
2\rightarrow 2
S-matrix will be given by a phase,
S=e^{i\delta(p_{i})}
, where
p_{i}
are the particles’ momenta, and this phase can be related to the ground state energy in finite-size,
E_{0}(R)
, via the TBA equations mentioned above. We will not use the full TBA equations herein but, following
[
30
]
, we will use a simpler argument to find the effect of the deformation on the S-matrix.

Consider a two-particle eigenstate of the Hamiltonian on the cylinder (e.g.,
a^{\dagger}_{-\mbox{\tiny{$N$}}}\tilde{a}^{\dagger}_{-\mbox{\tiny{$N$}}}|0\rangle
) with zero total momentum, so
\tilde{N}=N
. In the Schrödinger picture, the time evolution of this state is

|N,N,t\rangle=e^{-iE(N,N,R)\,t}|N,N,0\rangle

(3.56)

The same state can be thought of as describing two massless particles, one left-moving and one right-moving, which circle around the cylinder in opposite directions and interact every
\Delta t=R/2
. At each interaction, the two-particle wavefunction picks up a phase shift
e^{i\delta(p_{i})}
, which can be taken to be the flat space scattering phase if
R
is very large. It will be convenient to work in terms of the Mandelstam variable

s=(p_{1}+p_{2})^{2}=E_{cm}^{2}=\frac{16\pi^{2}N^{2}}{R^{2}}

(3.57)

The scattering phase only depends on the external momenta
p_{i}
though
s
due to the special
2d
kinematics. From the point of view of the two particles, the state at time
t\gg R
can be written as

|N,N,t\rangle=e^{-i(\Delta E(N,0,R)+\Delta E(0,N,R)+E(0,0,R))t+i\delta(s)2t/R}|N,N,0\rangle

(3.58)

where
\Delta E(N,\tilde{N},R)
is the energy of the respective state with respect to the vacuum, whose energy is
E(0,0,R)
. Equating the two exponents, we find that

\delta(s)=\lim_{R\rightarrow\infty}\frac{R}{2}(\Delta E(N,0,R)+\Delta E(0,N,R)+E(0,0,R)-E(N,N,R))

(3.59)

i.e., the scattering phase is proportional to the binding energy of the two-particle state in the large radius limit. As we send
R\rightarrow\infty
, we would like to keep the particles’ momenta,
2\pi N/R
fixed, which yields in the following result for
\delta(s)

\delta(s)=\frac{8\pi^{2}N^{2}\mu}{R^{2}}=\frac{\mu s}{2}

(3.60)

Thus, the exact S-matrix takes the extremely simple form

S=e^{i\mu s/2}

(3.61)

This expression can also be derived using the TBA equations
[
30
]
.

Despite its unusual form, the above S-matrix is perfectly consistent with the S-matrix axioms: unitarity, analyticity, crossing, as described in e.g.
[
137
]
. More precisely, it corresponds to a so-called CDD factor
[
136
]
, which generally takes the form

\Phi_{\{\mu\}}(\Delta\beta)=\exp\left(-i\sum_{k\in 2\mathbb{Z}+1}\mu_{k}\sinh(k\,\Delta\beta)\right)

(3.62)

where
\Delta\beta
is the relative rapidity of the two particles being scattered; see
[
137
]
for the corresponding massless expression, which involves a phase that is an odd polynomial in
s
. CDD factors represent ambiguities that arise in the
S
- matrix bootstrap approach, since they can be added (in a multiplicative fashion) to a consistent
2\rightarrow 2
scattering amplitude without violating the analyticity, unitarity and crossing symmetry conditions. In general, such terms are set to zero in order to avoid that the S-matrix behave non-polynomially as
s\rightarrow\infty
, which is associated with non-locality of the corresponding QFT.

In the
T\bar{T}
case, one is interested in precisely the non-local behaviour associated with the CDD phase factor, given by (
3.61
) for free bosons. In the sequel we show that, indeed, a theory with such an S-matrix
exhibits a minimum length that can be probed in scattering.

Physical manifestations of the scattering phase

In this subsection, which closely follows
[
30
]
, we discuss two important physical effects of the scattering phase, still in the context of the
T\bar{T}
-deformed free bosons: a universal time delay proportional to the energy - already prefigured by the classical analysis leading to (
3.49
) - and the presence of a minimum length.

To see them, we consider the scattering of two Gaussian wavepackets, one left-moving and one right-moving, with profile functions
f_{L}(p_{L})
and
f_{R}(p_{R})
given by
19
19

19

For
\bar{p}\gg\Delta p
, we can replace the integration range to be
(-\infty,\infty)
, instead of
(0,\infty)
; an overbar denotes an average in this subsection.

f_{L}(p_{L})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Delta p_{L}\sqrt{\pi}}}\exp\left(-\frac{(p_{L}-\bar{p}_{L})^{2}}{2(\Delta p_{L})^{2}}\right)

(3.63)

and similarly for
f_{R}
. The
in
state, prepared at
t\rightarrow-\infty
, takes the form

|in\rangle=\int_{0}^{\infty}dp_{L}dp_{R}f_{L}(p_{L})f_{R}(p_{R})\,a^{\dagger}(p_{L})\tilde{a}^{\dagger}(p_{R})|0\rangle

(3.64)

where
a^{\dagger}
and
\tilde{a}^{\dagger}
are the creation operators for left/right-moving modes. As
t\rightarrow\infty
, we have

|out\rangle=\int_{0}^{\infty}dp_{L}dp_{R}f_{L}(p_{L})f_{R}(p_{R})\,e^{2i\mu\,p_{L}p_{R}}\,a^{\dagger}(p_{L})\tilde{a}^{\dagger}(p_{R})|0\rangle

(3.65)

since
s=2\,\eta^{\alpha\beta}p_{L,\alpha}p_{R,\beta}=4\,p_{L}p_{R}
. The momentum-space reduced density matrix for the left-movers, obtained after tracing out the right-moving modes, is given by

\displaystyle\rho(p_{L},p_{L}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Tr_{RM}|out\rangle\langle out|=f(p_{L})f_{L}^{\star}(p_{L}^{\prime})\int_{0}^{\infty}dp_{R}|f_{R}(p_{R})|^{2}e^{2i\mu(p_{L}-p_{L}^{\prime})p_{R}}

(3.66)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(p_{L})f_{L}^{\star}(p_{L}^{\prime})\,e^{2i\mu(p_{L}-p_{L}^{\prime})\bar{p}_{R}-\mu^{2}(p_{L}-p_{L}^{\prime})^{2}\Delta p_{R}^{2}}

The probability density to find the left-moving wavepacket at some
x_{L}
(large and negative) for
t
large, is given by the Fourier transform of the above expression

\rho(x_{L},x_{L},t)=\int dp_{L}e^{-ip_{L}(t+x_{L})}\int dp^{\prime}_{L}e^{-ip^{\prime}_{L}(t+x_{L})}\rho(p_{L},p_{L}^{\prime})\approx e^{-\frac{(t+x_{L}-2\mu\bar{p}_{R})^{2}}{(\Delta x_{L})^{2}}}

(3.67)

where
\Delta x_{L}
is given by

(\Delta x_{L})^{2}=\mu^{2}(\Delta p_{R})^{2}+\frac{1}{4(\Delta p_{L})^{2}}

(3.68)

This answer has several rather interesting features. The first feature is the time delay: if in absence of the deformation, the probability would have peaked at
t\approx-x_{L}
, in its presence it peaks
later
by an amount
2\mu\bar{p}_{R}
, proportional to the energy of the right-moving wavepacket. This is very reminiscent of gravitational scattering, which also produces time delays proportional to the energy. It is the quantum version of the effect (
3.49
) we have seen earlier at classical level, which was induced by the field-dependent coordinate transformation. The reason this coordinate transformation can affect the S-matrix is that it is a ‘large’ coordinate transformation, which acts non-trivially on the asymptotic states of the theory.

The second very interesting effect is the existence of a minimum length. As we see from (
3.68
), the width of the wavepacket after the scattering is given by
\Delta x_{L}
, and there will be a similar expression for the post-scattering width

\Delta x_{R}
of the right-moving wavepacket. The two satisfy the inequality

\Delta x_{L}\Delta x_{R}\geq\mu

(3.69)

which was nicknamed a ‘stringy uncertainty principle’ in
[
30
]
, and indicates that it is impossible to resolve lengths smaller than
\sqrt{\mu}
. A third interesting effect one can already notice from the momentum-space expression (
3.66
) is that the off-diagonal matrix elements of the density matrix are suppressed, implying that the outgoing wavepackets are highly entangled with each other after the scattering. In
[
30
]
, this has been likened with a very toy version of black hole evaporation.

Thus, even though the S-matrix of
T\bar{T}
- deformed free bosons is an extremely simple phase, it encodes some surprisingly rich physics, which shows that the
T\bar{T}
deformation produces a non-local theory with certain gravitational properties. Whether this should be considered as a theory of quantum gravity in
2d
is unclear, not least because gravity in
2d
has no propagating degrees of freedom. Before moving on to more general
T\bar{T}
- deformed QFTs and exploring their link to
2d
gravitational theories, we would like to briefly review what happens to the link between
T\bar{T}
- deformed free bosons and the worldsheet theory of the bosonic string at the quantum level.

T\bar{T}
- deformed free bosons vs. strings in flat space: quantum analysis

As we saw in the previous subsection,
D-2

T\bar{T}
-deformed free bosons are classically equivalent to the Nambu-Goto action in
D
target space dimensions in static gauge. In the quantum theory, a few interesting and interrelated questions arise:

i)

the above identification implies that the
T\bar{T}
- deformed free boson theory possesses non-linearly realised target space Poincaré symmetry, even though only an
ISO(1,1)\times SO(D-2)
subgroup is manifest. What happens to this symmetry upon quantization?

ii)

the
T\bar{T}
- deformed free boson theory is UV-complete, as indicated by the exact S-matrix (
3.61
). This fact is not obvious from the string perspective, as the static-gauge Nambu-Goto action naïvely appears to correspond to a non-renormalizable theory, due to the presence of an infinite number of higher derivative terms. Of course, at least in the critical dimension
D=26
, this action describes the bosonic string and is thus UV-complete, but how about other dimensions?

The question whether the identity between
T\bar{T}
- deformed free bosons and strings in flat space survives at the quantum level was effectively addressed in
[
167
]
. From the
T\bar{T}
perspective, a failure of this relation would be seen as a breaking of the non-linearly realised
ISO(1,D-1)
, while integrability and the scattering amplitude (
3.61
) are preserved; from the perspective of the string - which must realise the target space isometries - it would be seen as a departure from (
3.61
), i.e. a breaking of integrability.

The authors studied the scattering of worldsheet excitations on an infinitely long string in
D
target space dimensions perturbatively in
\alpha^{\prime}=\ell_{s}^{2}
, taking an effective field theory approach. While this brute-force calculation becomes
quickly tedious
20
20

20

It has been carried up to four loops in
[
168
]
.
, and one can only reproduce a few terms in the expansion of the scattering phase (
3.61
), the usefulness of this exercise is that it renders more transparent the
various assumptions at play, the structure of divergences one encounters and the possible choices of
counterterms needed to subtract them, the role played by integrability in the definition of
T\bar{T}
, and what is special about the
critical dimension.

At low energies, the relevant degrees of freedom correspond to the
D-2
Goldstone bosons,
X^{i}
, associated to the translational symmetries broken by the presence of the string. The first term in the effective action is the Nambu-Goto area term; in static gauge about the long string background, one is thus studying the scattering of
D-2
massless bosons, with manifest
SO(D-2)
‘flavour’ symmetry, in this naïvely non-renormalizable theory.
The amplitude for scattering particles
i,j
into
k,l
takes the general form

\mathcal{M}_{ij\rightarrow kl}=A\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}+B\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}+C\delta_{il}\delta_{jk}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;\;i,j,k,l\in\{1,\ldots,D-2\}

(3.70)

where
A
is the amplitude for annihilation,
B
for transmission, and
C
for reflection. They are functions of the Mandelstam variables
s,t,u
and the string length
\ell_{s}
, and are related by crossing. Due to a peculiarity of
2d
kinematics, one has either
t=0
with
u=-s
, or
u=0
with
t=-s
.

Taking the ‘
T\bar{T}
perspective’, we note that
the formula (
3.55
), which represents the exact spectrum of
D-2

T\bar{T}
-deformed free bosons, indicates that states with the same level, but different
SO(D-2)
quantum numbers, are exactly degenerate. The interaction Hamiltonian is thus proportional to the identity on the degenerate subspace, from which one concludes that the annihilation part,
A
, of the amplitude should be zero. In
[
167
]
, the coefficients
A,B,C
above were computed up to one loop, using the derivative expansion of the Nambu-Goto action only and keeping track of divergences via dimensional regularization. It was found that

A_{tree}=0\;,\;\;\;\;\;B_{tree}=\frac{l_{s}^{2}}{2}s^{2}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;A_{1-loop}=-\frac{D-26}{192\pi}l_{s}^{4}s^{3}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;B_{1-loop}=i\frac{l_{s}^{4}}{16}s^{3}

(3.71)

while
C
can be determined by crossing.
Precisely in the critical dimension,
D=26
, the annihilation part of the amplitude vanishes and the Nambu-Goto action plus counterterms can equal the
T\bar{T}
- deformed bosons. The case
D=3
is special, as the index
i
only takes one value, so it only makes sense to talk about the analogous contribution to the full amplitude
A+B+C
, which does vanish. One can easily check that the transmission part of the amplitude,
B
, precisely equals the expansion of (
3.61
) with
\mu=\ell_{s}^{2}/2
up to this order
21
21

21

Taking into account normalisations, the relationship between the two is
e^{2i\delta(s)}=1+iB/(2s)
.
.

In all other dimensions, in order to have
A=0
, one needs to explicitly add to the action a counterterm that cancels the contribution of the so-called Polchinski-Strominger (PS) term
[
169
]
. To the given order in static gauge, this counterterm takes the form

S_{PS}=-\frac{D-26}{192\pi}\int d^{2}\sigma\,\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}X^{i}\partial^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}X^{i}\,\partial_{\gamma}X^{j}\partial^{\gamma}X^{j}

(3.72)

which explicitly breaks the non-linearly realised Lorentz symmetry. Thus, we conclude that the non-linearly realised Poincaré symmetry of the classical
T\bar{T}
- deformed action is
not
preserved at the quantum level, except for
D=3,26
. In all other dimensions,
the
T\bar{T}
deformation of
D-2
free bosons is just an integrable theory, whose exact S-matrix is given by (
3.61
) and is associated, via integrability, to the exact spectrum (
3.55
). The action that perturbatively reproduces this amplitude is

S_{NG}+S_{ct}+S_{PS}
, which also contains the higher order analogues of the Lorentz-breaking PS term required to cancel the Nambu-Goto contribution to the annihilation amplitude up to the desired order. As for the counterterms, one needs ever new ones as the loop level is increased (an infinite number of them), and the only rule that appears to fix their finite part is to require integrability, i.e. that the scattering amplitude they predict match the result (
3.61
) predicted from integrability. In principle, one could ask whether the SZ definition (
3.3
) can be used to perturbatively build the action using the renormalised stress tensor (in some chosen scheme) at the previous order; to our knowledge, this question has not been addressed in the literature.

Thus, one sees that integrability provides a guide for how to incorporate ever higher-derivative terms to the
D-2
free boson action in order to produce a UV-complete theory, albeit one with a non-standard UV behaviour indicative of short-range non-locality. While integrability appears essential for being able to define the theory, it is an interesting question whether this UV behaviour can be maintained while slightly breaking the integrable structure.

Requiring integrability for any
D
at the expense of the target space Lorentz symmetry is natural from the
T\bar{T}
point of view. However, one is sometimes interested in an effective string description of, e.g., the QCD string, for which the Nambu-Goto action provides the departure point. In that case, one would instead like to preserve the non-linearly realized Lorentz symmetry, and therefore does not add the
PS
counterterm (
3.72
). Assuming approximate integrability, the effective action can be inferred, to low orders, by comparison to lattice QCD data on the finite-size spectrum
[
170
,
171
,
172
]
; interestingly, evidence is found for an additional light, but massive excitation on the string worldsheet.
An interesting question is whether such a theory, which is not exactly integrable, may nonetheless exhibit asymptotically fragile behaviour at high energies, as suggested by the very nature of the underlying string. This question is most meaningfully formulated in the planar limit of the gauge theory, where the string worldsheet description applies in a large range of energies
[
173
]
.

In the following, we return to
T\bar{T}
and address the obvious question whether more general
T\bar{T}
- deformed QFTs also exhibit a universal asymptotic behaviour for their S-matrix. If the original QFT is integrable, then this question can be approached using TBA. In the general case, one needs to resort to an alternate, non-perturbative definition of
T\bar{T}
- deformed QFTs put forth in
[
139
,
174
]
.

Universal
T\bar{T}
deformation of an integrable S-matrix via TBA

The Thermodynamic Bethe Ansatz (TBA) reasoning that allowed us to derive the scattering phase for the
T\bar{T}
- deformed free bosons using their known spectrum can be easily generalised to arbitrary
T\bar{T}
- deformed integrable QFTs. To have a general, well-defined S-matrix, we concentrate on massive QFTs.

Consider the scattering of massive particles with mass
m
(taken to be the same, for simplicity) in an integrable QFT. It is convenient to write the particles’ momenta in terms of the rapidities,
\beta

p^{0}_{i}=m\cosh\beta_{i}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;p^{1}_{i}=m\sinh\beta_{i}

(3.73)

In the integrable case, the
2\rightarrow 2
S-matrix is a pure phase,
S=e^{i\delta(\beta_{i})}
. As beautifully explained in
[
165
]
,
this scattering phase is related to the finite-size spectrum of the theory via the so-called TBA equations, which we now briefly review.

As a first step, one
considers the partition function of the
2d
QFT on a torus of size
(L,R)
, where
L\gg R
. Depending on which torus direction is interpreted as
euclidean time, this partition function can be approximated as either the ground state energy in finite volume, or as the finite-temperature free energy in infinite volume

Z(L,R)\approx e^{-LE_{0}(R)}\approx e^{-Rf(R)L}

(3.74)

which leads to an equality between the two quantities in the exponent.

In the second step,
the free energy is estimated by noting that in approximately infinite volume, one has a well-defined notion of asymptotic states, and doing statistics over a large number of particles that scatter. The scattering phase
\delta(p_{i},p_{j})
enters in the quantization condition for their momenta as

mL\sinh\beta_{i}+\sum_{j\neq i}\delta(\beta_{i},\beta_{j})=2\pi n_{i}\;,\;\;\;\;\;n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}

(3.75)

In the limit of a large number of particles, it is convenient to introduce the particle and level densities, which are constrained by the above equation. The free energy is computed using usual thermodynamic considerations and then minimized. Specializing to the ‘bosonic’ case, at the minimum one finds

E_{0}(R)=Rf(R)=\frac{m}{2\pi}\int d\beta\cosh\beta\ln(1-e^{-\varepsilon(\beta)})

(3.76)

where the ‘pseudoenergy’
\varepsilon(\beta)
is constrained to obey

\varepsilon(\beta)=mR\cosh\beta+\frac{1}{2\pi}\int d\beta^{\prime}\frac{\partial\delta(\beta^{\prime},\beta)}{\partial\beta^{\prime}}\ln(1-e^{-\varepsilon(\beta^{\prime})})

(3.77)

This provides the advertised TBA relation between the finite-size ground state energy and the scattering phase. This relation can also be generalised to excited states
[
175
]
.

Let us now turn to the
T\bar{T}
deformation of an integrable QFT. The scattering phase
\delta^{[0]}(\beta^{\prime},\beta)
of the undeformed QFT satisfies the TBA equations for some pseudoenergy
\varepsilon^{[0]}
. Let us assume, for simplicity, that the momentum
P=m\int d\beta\sinh\beta\ln(1-e^{-\varepsilon(\beta)})=0
. After the deformation, the pseudoenergy takes on a new value, determined by the change in the scattering phase, which in turn determines the modification of the ground state energy. Since the latter satisfies
E_{0}^{[\mu]}(R)=E_{0}^{[0]}(R+\mu E_{0}^{[\mu]})
, we see that the deformed pseudoenergy should satisfy (
3.77
) with the same
\delta^{[0]}(\beta^{\prime},\beta)
, but with the radius shifted as

R\rightarrow R+\mu E_{0}^{[\mu]}
.
E_{0}^{[\mu]}
is given by (
3.76
), computed with the deformed pseudoenergy. From the explicit expression (
3.77
), it is easy to see that the shift in the radius can be traded for a shift in the scattering phase that satisfies
\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}\Delta\delta(\beta^{\prime},\beta)=\mu m^{2}\cosh\beta^{\prime}\cosh\beta
, up to terms proportional to
\sinh\beta^{\prime}
that integrate to zero, by assumption. This implies that the scattering phase in the deformed theory takes the form

\delta^{[\mu]}(\beta_{i})=\delta^{[0]}(\beta_{i})+\Delta\delta(\beta_{i})\;,\;\;\;\;\;\Delta\delta(\beta^{\prime},\beta)=\mu m^{2}\sinh(\beta^{\prime}-\beta)=\mu\,\epsilon_{ab}\,p^{a}p^{\prime b}

(3.78)

with
\epsilon_{01}=1
.
Thus, the
T\bar{T}
deformation leads to a universal shift in the scattering phase, given by (
3.78
), which is linked to the universal
T\bar{T}
deformation of the spectrum as we just explained.
A generalization of the TBA argument to non-zero momentum and to excited states is also possible
[
29
]
, with the same result for
\Delta\delta
.

One recognises that the scattering phase (
3.78
) corresponds to the CDD factor (
3.62
) with only the
k=1
term non-zero. Thus, also in the massive case, we find that the S-matrix is multiplied by a factor that is fully consistent with the S-matrix bootstrap equations. Assuming the original QFT was UV-complete (with the UV behaviour governed by a CFT), the exact expression for the
T\bar{T}
- deformed S-matrix indicates the deformed theory will also be UV-complete, albeit with a non-local behaviour in the UV. For an arbitrary factorisable scattering process, the phases add up, and the deformed S-matrix reads

\mathcal{S}^{[\mu]}(p_{i})=e^{i\mu\sum_{i<j}\epsilon_{ab}p_{i}^{a}p_{j}^{b}}\,\mathcal{S}^{[0]}(p_{i})

(3.79)

where we have taken all particles to be incoming, and ordered according to their rapidities.

Therefore, we see that, in integrable theories, the effect of the
T\bar{T}
deformation on the S-matrix is to simply multiply it by the above universal phase factor.
It is interesting to ask what is the effect of the
T\bar{T}
deformation on the S-matrix of an arbitrary
2d
QFT, which need not be integrable. Of course, one can simply define
[
31
]
the deformed S-matrix via the dressing procedure above but, in absence of integrability, its relation to the
T\bar{T}
- deformed QFT is unclear. In the following, we describe an alternate definition of the
T\bar{T}
deformation that is able to reproduce both the universally-deformed spectrum and the universal modification of the S-matrix, and applies to general QFTs.

Non-perturbative definition of the
T\bar{T}
deformation

The definition (
3.3
) of the
T\bar{T}
deformation discussed at the beginning of this section has the advantage of being based on an action, and allows one to compute the exact spectrum of the deformed QFT; however, this definition only makes sense at distances much longer than the non-locality scale
\sqrt{\mu}
, where the QFT can be treated quasi-locally and one is able to associate a stress tensor to the translational symmetries.

The fact that the deformed QFT could actually be UV complete was instead seen, at least for the case of integrable QFTs, from the exact expression (
3.79
) for the S-matrix ((
3.61
) in the massless case), which is well-defined up to arbitrarily high energies. Given this, one may be lead
[
31
]
to provide an alternative definition of the
T\bar{T}
deformation, based on its effect on the S-matrix
[
31
]
.
This S-matrix-based definition makes the UV completeness manifest; it nonetheless has the drawback of not being based on an action principle.

In the following, we briefly sketch how these two approaches can be unified via a general, path-integral definition of the
T\bar{T}
deformation of
arbitrary
QFTs on flat space, which is able to reproduce both the deformed spectrum and the deformed S-matrix.
This was achieved via a particular coupling of the original quantum
field theory to a special theory of topological gravity
[
174
]

Z_{T\bar{T}}[\mu]=\int\mathcal{D}X^{a}\mathcal{D}e^{a}_{\alpha}\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2\mu}\int d^{2}x\,e\,\epsilon_{ab}\,\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}(\partial_{\alpha}X^{a}-e^{a}_{\alpha})(\partial_{\beta}X^{b}-e^{b}_{\beta})+S_{QFT}(e^{a}_{\alpha})\right]

(3.80)

where
e^{a}_{\alpha}
is a nontrivial background vielbein and the
X^{a}
are a pair of auxiliary fields whose equations of
motion set
\partial_{[\alpha}e^{a}_{\beta]}=0
, implying that the curvature is exactly zero. This is important for the quantum
theory and is ultimately responsible for the solvability of this deformation, as the semiclassical sum over
topologies only receives a contribution from the torus (in the compact case), with the higher-genus Riemann surfaces being
precluded by the Gauss-Bonnet theorem. The absence of a sum over topologies in the above definition suggests that
T\bar{T}
-deformed QFTs are more like non-local
2d
QFTs than theories of two-dimensional quantum gravity, despite the presence of many gravitational features.

The above definition can be justified by treating the
T\bar{T}
deformation using the Hubbard-Stratonovich method
[
176
]
, which in this case amounts to coupling the original QFT to a dynamical metric
22
22

22

This formulation is also the departure point for understanding the holographic interpretation of the
T\bar{T}
deformation.
. However, as shown in that work, the conservation of the stress tensor implies that the path integral reduces to one only over flat metrics, at least infinitesimally. When passing from metrics to vielbeine, a simple way to enforce this constraint is to introduce the auxiliary fields
X^{a}
as above, whose equations of motion impose the flatness condition
\partial_{[\alpha}e^{a}_{\beta]}=0
.
The vielbein equations of motion impose

\partial_{\alpha}X^{a}=e^{a}_{\alpha}+\mu\,\epsilon^{ab}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}T^{\beta}_{b}

(3.81)

which is nothing but a generalization of the field-dependent coordinate transformation from static
(\partial_{\alpha}X^{a}=\delta^{a}_{\alpha})
to conformal gauge (
e^{a}_{\alpha}=\delta^{a}_{\alpha}
) that we have seen before in the Nambu-Goto action.
This equation points towards an interpretation of the
T\bar{T}
deformation as providing a set of dynamical coordinates,
X^{a}
, through which the underlying QFT dynamics is seen
[
160
]
.

By computing the torus partition function using the above definition,
[
174
]
were able to derive a flow equation for the partition function
23
23

23

The flow equation can also be found using the metric formulation
[
176
]
.
, which precisely reproduces the flow equation (
3.15
) on the energy levels. Concommitently, in
[
139
]
it was shown that the effect of coupling to the non-dynamical vielbein precisely reproduces the dressing (
3.79
) on the S-matrix. Thus, the path integral above is indeed able to unify the two previous definitions of the
T\bar{T}
deformation.

3.3
The
J\bar{T}
and
JT^{a}
deformations

The
J\bar{T}
deformation
[
132
]
is another very well-studied Smirnov-Zamolodchikov deformation. As its name indicates, the deforming operator is constructed from the components of a
U(1)
current,
J
, and those of the generator of right-moving translations,
T_{\alpha\bar{z}}

\mathcal{O}_{J\bar{T}}=\lim_{x^{\prime}\rightarrow x}\left(J_{z}(x^{\prime})T_{\bar{z}\bar{z}}(x)-J_{\bar{z}}(x^{\prime})T_{z\bar{z}}(x)\right)+...

(3.82)

which only has a
\bar{T}\equiv T_{\bar{z}\bar{z}}
component when considered in a CFT.

Since the
J\bar{T}
operator has spin one, the corresponding deformation breaks Lorentz invariance, which in turn implies that the deformed stress tensor is no longer symmetric. As for all SZ deformations, the change in the action is given by

\frac{\partial S}{\partial\lambda}=\int d^{2}x\,\mathcal{O}_{J\bar{T}}(\lambda)

(3.83)

We have denoted the
J\bar{T}
coupling constant by
\lambda
, to differentiate it from the
T\bar{T}
coupling. It has dimensions of length and, due to the non-zero spin of the
J\bar{T}
operator, should best be thought as a vector
\lambda^{a}=\lambda\delta^{a}_{\bar{z}}
that points in the right-moving direction (which is null in Lorentzian signature). One can similarly define the
JT^{a}
deformation, constructed from a
U(1)
current and the components of the translation generator in the direction
\hat{x}^{a}
,
T_{\alpha a}
- whose coupling constant is a fixed, but arbitrary vector
\lambda^{a}
- and has very similar properties to
J\bar{T}
. As in the
T\bar{T}
case, these theories are expected to be UV - complete.

One special feature of the
J\bar{T}
deformation that distinguishes it from the generic
JT^{a}
ones is that it preserves an
SL(2,\mathbb{R})
subroup of the original conformal group if the seed theory that is being deformed is a CFT. This follows from the fact that the
J\bar{T}
operator has dimension
(1,2)
, i.e., it is exactly marginal on the left, whereas a general
JT^{a}
one contains both
(1,2)
and
(2,1)
contributions. Consequently, a
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFT will be
local
and
conformal
on the left-moving side, and all its non-locality will be concentrated along the null right-moving direction. Note these are precisely the properties expected of a two-dimensional
dipole CFT
, introduced in section
2
, which is a UV-complete QFT that can be obtained via a series of
SL(2,\mathbb{R})_{L}
- invariant irrelevant deformations of a
2d
CFT, starting with a
(1,2)
operator. Our main motivation for studying
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs is thus that

\star

J\bar{T}
-deformed CFTs represent the first concrete, tractable example of a two-dimensional dipole CFT

\star

As per our discussion in section
2
, they consequently represent the first solvable toy model for the Kerr/‘CFT’ correspondence. Of course, this is not to mean that extremal black holes are actually described by
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs; the claim is simply that
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs are the first concrete example of QFTs whose structure (i.e., the not-yet-formalized axioms that dipole CFTs obey) may be the same as that of theories holographically describing near-extremal black holes. As such, one may hope they will shed light on some of the puzzles raised in the Kerr/‘CFT’ or, rather, warped AdS
3
context, such as the presence of Virasoro symmetry and CFT-like correlation functions in what appears to be a non-local theory. We will show that
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs can, indeed, offer an interesting perspective on these issues; see section
5
and, respectively,
6
. As we will see, the ‘half-local’ and conformal structure of
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs make the study of precisely these type of observables much simpler than in
T\bar{T}
, which is a fully non-local theory.

In the current section, we will only concentrate on very basic properties of
J\bar{T}
and
JT^{a}
-deformed QFTs that follow from their special status as Smirnov-Zamolodchikov deformations and are directly analogous to the
T\bar{T}
properties we previously studied: connection to integrability, universal deformation of the spectrum and of the S-matrix, non-perturbative definition by coupling to topological background fields.
While in the
T\bar{T}
case, there was a strong physical motivation - the connection to (lattice QCD data on) the confining QCD string - to be studying the finite-size spectrum and its relation to the S-matrix via TBA, for
J\bar{T}
the most interesting physics application is to holography where, as explained, the most relevant observables are the symmetries and the form of correlation functions
24
24

24

The reason is that these observables are expected to be (more) universal, whereas the finite-size spectrum is not.
.

Before we enter the details, let us make a technical remark regarding the computation of the finite-size spectrum of
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs.
In the original formulation
[
132
]
of the
J\bar{T}
deformation, the
U(1)
current was restricted to be chiral, which was believed to lead to important simplifications.
However, these apparent simplifications led to a number of subtleties in the quantum analysis, due to anomalies. As it turns out, the theories defined with a chiral versus non-chiral
J
are in many ways equivalent, and for many purposes it is conceptually clearer to simply take
J
non-chiral. In what concerns the spectrum computation, the correct general way to approach it is by studying the deformed theory in presence of constant background fields
[
133
]
, case in which there is no particular simplification to considering
J\bar{T}
vs.
JT^{a}
, or CFT vs. QFT; we will present this computation towards the end of this subsection. For the special case of
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs with
J
chiral, there turns out to exist a much simpler, indirect argument for obtaining the correct spectrum
[
142
]
, which we now present.

I. Spectrum - take one - and thermodynamics

In this subsection, we present a simple - if slightly indirect - derivation of the spectrum of a
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFT on the cylinder. The current
J
is assumed to be chiral and the steps in the argument closely follow
those used for deriving the
T\bar{T}
flow equation for the spectrum. A more complete and general analysis can be found at the end of this subsection.

The spectrum of
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs on a cylinder

As explained in section
3.1
, under a SZ deformation the energy levels
E_{n}(\lambda,R)
of the system put on a cylinder will be changing as

\frac{\partial E_{n}^{[\lambda]}(R)}{\partial\lambda}=2R\,\left(\langle n|J_{z}|n\rangle\langle n|T_{\bar{z}\bar{z}}|n\rangle-\langle n|J_{\bar{z}}|n\rangle\langle n|T_{z\bar{z}}|n\rangle\right)

(3.84)

where we simply plugged in the specific
J\bar{T}
deformation into (
3.7
) and replaced
\mu
by
\lambda
. As in
[
27
,
29
]
, this will be the essential equation allowing us to compute the exact finite-size spectrum of the deformed theory, provided we can relate the expectation values of the current components in the energy eigenstate to conserved charges.

For the stress tensor, this is a rather simple task. The expectation values of
T_{\tau\tau},T_{\tau\sigma}
and
T_{\sigma\sigma}
in the translationally-invariant state
|n\rangle
are, as before, related to the corresponding conserved charges as in (
3.14
).
A priori, this leaves undetermined the
\sigma\tau
component of the stress tensor which, due to the broken Lorentz invariance, no longer equals the
\tau\sigma
one. However, this problem can be circumvented in the case of
J\bar{T}
- deformed
CFTs
, if one assumes that the
SL(2,\mathbb{R})_{L}
symmetry is preserved at the full quantum level, which requires that

T_{\bar{z}z}=\frac{1}{4}[T_{\sigma\sigma}+T_{\tau\tau}+i(T_{\tau\sigma}-T_{\sigma\tau})]=0

(3.85)

thus determining the
T_{\sigma\tau}
component in terms of the rest.
To solve the flow equation, one also needs the current components. As usual, the time component of the current is related to the conserved charge as

\langle n|J_{\tau}|n\rangle=\frac{iQ_{n}}{R}

(3.86)

but we still need an expression for
\langle n|J_{\varphi}|n\rangle
. There are two ways of proceeding: one is to make the additional simplifying assumption that the current is purely holomorphic, as in
[
132
,
142
]
; the other, following
[
133
,
134
]
is to compute
\langle n|J_{\varphi}|n\rangle
by coupling to background gauge potential. In this subsection, we will follow the first path, which only applies to seed CFTs, but is also much simpler.

Assuming that the current is purely holomorphic, which implies
J_{\varphi}=-iJ_{\tau}
in Euclidean signature, and plugging into the flow equation for the energies, we find
Factors of 2!

\frac{\partial E_{n}^{[\lambda]}(R)}{\partial\lambda}=-Q_{L,n}\left(\frac{\partial E_{n}}{\partial R}+\frac{P_{n}}{R}\right)

(3.87)

which is the equation that we need to solve. The subscript ‘
L
’ that we placed on
Q
indicates it is the charge associated with the chiral left-moving current.
Since
P_{n}R\in\mathbb{Z}
,
P_{n}
cannot vary with
\lambda
, while its
R
- dependence is fixed. It is useful to introduce the left/right-moving energies
E_{L,R}
via

E=E_{L}+E_{R}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;P=E_{L}-E_{R}

(3.88)

where we dropped the subscript
n
on the eigenstates. In terms of these, the flow equation simplifies to
\partial_{\lambda}E_{R}=-Q_{L}\partial_{R}E_{R}.
To solve it, we need to know how the left-moving charge depends on
\lambda
. As it turns out, if we insist upon chirality of
J
and that the level of the
U(1)
is non-zero (as we generically expect it to be in a non-trivial theory) then, due to the chiral anomaly, the charge starts flowing with
\lambda
.

To properly understand this flow, one would need a better grasp on how the current behaves in conformal perturbation theory. There is, however, a rigorous
indirect
argument for obtaining the modified spectrum, given in
[
142
]
. The argument is based upon splitting the left-moving stress tensor into a contribution from the chiral current, which takes the Sugawara form, and an independent ‘coset’ contribution, whose OPE with the current vanishes along the flow.
[
142
]
then argue that the coset part is unaffected by the deformation, which implies a spectral-flow type equation for the left-moving energy
25
25

25

Our notation is as follows:
E_{L/R}
, which should be written as
E_{L/R}^{[\lambda]}
, for consistency, are the left/right-moving energies in the deformed theory; we dropped the superscript to unclutter the notation. Same comment applies to
Q_{L}
.
J_{0}
, which should have been written as
Q_{L}^{[0]}
, is the charge of the left chiral current in the undeformed CFT; when a right-moving current will be considered,
\bar{J}_{0}
will be the corresponding right chiral current, and the winding will be defined as
w=J_{0}-\bar{J}_{0}
.

E_{L}R-\frac{2\pi Q_{L}^{2}}{k}=const=E_{L}^{[0]}R-\frac{2\pi J_{0}^{2}}{k}\equiv\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{L}

(3.89)

where
E_{L}^{[0]}R
is related to the left conformal dimension as in (
3.18
) and
J_{0}
is the undeformed chiral charge, which we assume to be quantized. Using constancy of
P=E_{L}-E_{R}
along the flow, one finds that also
E_{R}R-2\pi Q_{L}^{2}/k=const
. Taking a
\lambda
derivative and using the fact that the only dimensionless parameter in the problem is
\lambda/R
, which implies that
E_{R}=R^{-1}f(\lambda/R)
, one finds the following equation for the flow of
Q_{L}

\partial_{\lambda}Q_{L}=-\frac{k}{4\pi}R\,\partial_{R}E_{R}=\frac{k}{4\pi}(E_{R}+\lambda\,\partial_{\lambda}E_{R})=\frac{k}{4\pi}\partial_{\lambda}(\lambda E_{R})

(3.90)

Consequently, one finds that the charge varies along the flow as

Q_{L}=J_{0}+\frac{\lambda k}{4\pi}E_{R}

(3.91)

Plugging in the expression for
Q_{L}
, the solution for the right-moving energy in terms of the original CFT data is

E_{R}=\frac{4\pi}{\lambda^{2}k}\left(R-\lambda J_{0}-\sqrt{\left(R-\lambda J_{0}\right)^{2}-\frac{\lambda^{2}kR}{2\pi}E_{R}^{[0]}}\right)

(3.92)

which can also be written in terms of
h_{R}
using (
3.18
).
The expression for
E_{L}
follows from momentum conservation

E_{L}=E_{R}+\frac{2\pi(h_{L}-h_{R})}{R}

(3.93)

Plugging (
3.92
) into (
3.91
), the expression for the chiral charge
Q_{L}
is given by

Q_{L}=\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(R-\sqrt{(R-\lambda J_{0})^{2}-\lambda^{2}k\left(h_{R}-\frac{c}{24}\right)}\right)

(3.94)

Let us now comment on the properties of this spectrum. One immediately notes that when
h_{R}
/
E_{R}^{[0]}
becomes large at fixed
J_{0}
, the deformed energies become imaginary, a feature reminiscent of the behaviour of the finite-size spectrum of
T\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs with
\mu<0
. Note that quantum effects, captured by the anomaly coefficient
k
, are very important for reaching this conclusion.

The interpretation of the imaginary energy states is the same as that put forth in
[
147
]
for
\mu<0

T\bar{T}
: the propagation speed in backgrounds with large
h_{R}
exceeds the speed of light
26
26

26

Note that superluminal propagation does not in itself pose a problem, as the deformed theory is not Lorentz invariant.
, leading to the formation of CTCs in finite size; the imaginary energies are a direct manifestation of this inconsistency. To show the CTCs occur at precisely the expected energy, one needs to use the fact (discussed later in this section), that the
J\bar{T}
dynamics can also be reduced to that of the original CFT in terms of the field-dependent coordinates
27
27

27

Note the transformation
V\rightarrow V-\lambda\phi(U)
would be a symmetry of warped CFTs, though it is not a symmetry here.

u=U\;,\;\;\;\;\;v=V-\lambda\phi

(3.95)

where
\phi
is the bosonisation of the
U(1)
current. On a background characterised by a large chiral charge
Q_{L}
, we have
28
28

28

Even in the non-chiral case, we have
\phi=Q_{L}U/R-Q_{R}v/R_{v}
, which leads to the same
\Delta V
.

\phi=Q_{L}U/R
. Assuming propagation is on the CFT
v=V-\lambda\phi=const
lines, the accumulated time advance on such a background is

\Delta t=\frac{1}{2}(\Delta U-\Delta V)=\frac{\Delta U}{2R}(R-\lambda Q_{L})

(3.96)

which becomes negative if
Q_{L}>R/\lambda
(note
Q_{L}
increases as
E_{R}^{[0]}
is raised, at fixed
J_{0}
). Using (
3.94
), we see the CTC limit is reached precisely when the square root vanishes, i.e. at the onset of the imaginary energy states.

Given that
Q_{L}(\lambda)
is bounded above by
R/\lambda
, it may be more natural to keep this deformed charge fixed instead as
h_{R}
is taken to be large.
Reality of
Q_{L}
requires that
\hat{h}_{R}
lie below the parabola
(R/\lambda-J_{0})^{2}
. As can be seen from figure
17
, in which
q^{[0]}=J_{0}
, this still allows access to infinite energies, even if one imposes a BPS or a cosmic censorship bound (blue parabola) on the right-moving energy. Thus, the situation is better than in
\mu<0

T\bar{T}
, where only states very close to being extremal can be followed to high energies, and there is little sense to be made of an asymptotic formula for the entropy.

As will turn out, even in the case when
J
is not chiral from the outset, it will be possible to construct a chiral current out of it and the stress tensor. Moreover, the deformation of the theory appears to be the same whether it is driven by the original, non-chiral current, or this chiral derivate. As a result, the flow equation for the spectrum will always take the form (
3.87
), and there will be again a spectral flow on the left energies. Allowing for potentially different initial left/right-moving charges, it follows that the right-moving energies also satisfy a spectral flow-like equation, of the form

E_{R}\big(R-\lambda(J_{0}-\bar{J}_{0})\big)-\frac{2\pi}{k}\left(\bar{J}_{0}+\frac{k\lambda}{4\pi}E_{R}\right)^{2}=RE_{R}^{[0]}-\frac{2\pi}{k}\bar{J}_{0}^{2}\equiv\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{R}

(3.97)

This simply follows by adding
P
to the flow equation (
3.89
) for the left energy, and requiring that the ‘initial’ value for the right-moving charge be in principle different from that for the left-movers. Note, however, that the solution for
E_{R}
is still given by (
3.92
), which does not depend on
\bar{J}_{0}
. This result will be derived in full generality at the end of this section. It will be convenient to introduce the notation

R_{v}\equiv R-\lambda(J_{0}-\bar{J}_{0})=R-\lambda w

(3.98)

which corresponds to the radius of the field-dependent coordinate
v
above, where
w
is the winding of the scalar field around the compact direction.

Figure 17:
Range of undeformed right-moving energies (shaded region) that lead to real energies in
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs and are allowed by the cosmic censorship bound on the left/right-moving energy (green/blue parabola). Note this range extends to infinite energy. The region becomes larger if we use instead the BPS bounds.

Thermodynamics

As in the
T\bar{T}
case, the entropy of
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs follows from the fact that
the spectrum is smoothly deformed, so we expect that the degeneracy still equals given by the degeneracy in the undeformed CFT
29
29

29

This is because the levels of fixed
h_{R}
and
J_{0}
do not cross as we vary
\lambda
.
written
in terms of the deformed energies (
3.92
) - (
3.93
).

Taking into account the fact that now there is charge, the undeformed CFT degeneracy is given in terms of the charged Cardy formula

S_{ch.\,Cardy}=\sqrt{\frac{\pi c}{3}}\bigg(\sqrt{RE_{L}^{[0]}-\frac{2\pi}{k}J_{0}^{2}}+\sqrt{RE_{R}^{[0]}-\frac{2\pi}{k}\bar{J}_{0}^{2}}\bigg)

(3.99)

where
E_{L,R}^{[0]}
are related to the CFT conformal dimensions via the usual relation (
3.18
). We immediately note that the quantities under the two square roots are nothing but the spectral flow invariants
\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{L,R}
(
3.89
) and (
3.97
), which do not change under the
J\bar{T}
flow. Consequently, the entropy can be written in terms of the
J\bar{T}
- deformed energies and charges as

S=\sqrt{\frac{\pi c}{3}}\bigg(\sqrt{RE_{L}-\frac{2\pi}{k}Q_{L}^{2}}+\sqrt{R_{v}E_{R}-\frac{2\pi}{k}Q_{R}^{2}}\bigg)

(3.100)

where
Q_{L,R}
are related to the charges
J_{0},\bar{J}_{0}
in the undeformed CFT - which are assumed to be integer-quantized - via

\displaystyle Q_{L}

\displaystyle=J_{0}+\frac{\lambda k}{4\pi}E_{R}\hskip 28.45274ptQ_{R}=\bar{J}_{0}+\frac{\lambda k}{4\pi}E_{R}

(3.101)

For now, the second equation can be considered a definition of
Q_{R}
. We will give a more appropriate one in the sequel.
We remark that the difference between the
J\bar{T}
- deformed charged Cardy formula and its CFT counterpart is rather minimal, if we write the former in terms of the deformed charges
Q_{L,R}
; indeed, the only difference is the factor of
R_{v}=R-\lambda Q_{L}+\lambda Q_{R}
. Things stand rather differently, of course, if we have access to the charge quantization condition, and interpret (
3.100
) in terms of
J_{0},\bar{J}_{0}
, case in which the difference from the CFT formula is significant. Of course, in this case one also needs to take into account the discussion of the previous section, regarding the fact that at fixed
J_{0}
,
E_{R}
will eventually become imaginary, so the regime of large
E_{R}
is not reached.

The relation between the left/right-moving energies and their associated temperatures
T_{L,R}
depends on which set of charges one holds fixed in the deformed theory. Assuming
J_{0},\bar{J}_{0}
are fixed, the first law of thermodynamics

\displaystyle\delta S

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T_{L}}\delta E_{L}+\frac{1}{T_{R}}\delta E_{R}+\mu_{L}\delta J_{0}+\mu_{R}\delta\bar{J}_{0}

(3.102)

allows us to compute
the temperatures and chemical potentials as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T_{L/R}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial S}{\partial E_{L/R}}\bigg|_{J_{0},\bar{J}_{0},E_{R/L}=fixed}\hskip 14.22636pt\mu_{L}=\frac{\partial S}{\partial J_{0}}\bigg|_{E_{L,R},\bar{J}_{0}=fixed},\hskip 14.22636pt\mu_{R}=\frac{\partial S}{\partial\bar{J}_{0}}\bigg|_{E_{L,R},J_{0}=fixed}

(3.103)

The undeformed thermodynamic potentials are related to the
J\bar{T}
flow invariants
\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{L/R}
and the charges as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T_{L/R}^{[0]}}=\sqrt{\frac{\pi c}{12}}\frac{R}{\sqrt{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{L/R}}}\;,\hskip 19.91684pt\mu_{L}^{[0]}=-\sqrt{\frac{\pi c}{12}}\frac{4\pi}{k}\frac{J_{0}}{\sqrt{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{L}}}\;,\hskip 19.91684pt\mu_{R}^{[0]}=-\sqrt{\frac{\pi c}{12}}\frac{4\pi}{k}\frac{\bar{J}_{0}}{\sqrt{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{R}}}

(3.104)

Using the expressions (
3.101
) for the
U(1)
charges
and the
J\bar{T}
flow invariants
\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{L,R}
, the deformed thermodynamic potentials evaluate to
[
144
]

\frac{1}{T_{L}}=\sqrt{\frac{\pi c}{12}}\frac{R}{\sqrt{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{L}}}=\frac{1}{T_{L}^{[0]}}\;,\hskip 20.20164pt\frac{1}{T_{R}}=\sqrt{\frac{\pi c}{12}}\bigg(\frac{R-\lambda Q_{L}}{\sqrt{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{R}}}-\frac{\lambda Q_{L}}{\sqrt{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{L}}}\bigg)=\frac{R-\lambda Q_{L}}{RT_{R}^{[0]}}-\frac{\lambda Q_{L}}{RT_{L}^{[0]}}

(3.105)

and

\displaystyle\mu_{L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\pi c}{12}}\bigg(-\frac{4\pi Q_{L}/k}{\sqrt{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{L}}}-\frac{\lambda E_{R}}{\sqrt{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{R}}}\bigg)=\mu_{L}^{[0]}-\frac{\lambda E_{R}}{R}\bigg(\frac{1}{T_{L}^{[0]}}+\frac{1}{T_{R}^{[0]}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\mu_{R}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\pi c}{12}}\bigg(\frac{\lambda E_{R}-\frac{4\pi}{k}Q_{R}}{\sqrt{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{R}}}\bigg)=-\sqrt{\frac{\pi c}{12}}\frac{4\pi}{k}\frac{\bar{J}_{0}}{\sqrt{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{R}}}=\mu_{R}^{[0]}

(3.106)

where for the second set of equalities we used (
3.104
).

To obtain the corresponding thermodynamic quantities for the chiral case with fixed
J_{0}
, we simply set
\bar{J}_{0}=0
in the formulae above. Again, one should be careful about the fact that the deformed energies become imaginary at fixed
J_{0}
. One can alternatively consider the ensemble with fixed
Q_{L}
and
\bar{J}_{0}
(with
\bar{J}_{0}=0
in the chiral case). The entropy (
3.100
) then reads

S=\sqrt{\frac{\pi c}{3}}\bigg(\sqrt{RE_{L}-\frac{2\pi}{k}Q_{L}^{2}}+\sqrt{(R-\lambda Q_{L})E_{R}+\frac{\lambda^{2}k}{8\pi}E_{R}^{2}-\frac{2\pi}{k}\bar{J}_{0}^{2}}\bigg)

(3.107)

which displays an interesting Hagedorn behaviour (for the right-movers only) in the regime of large
E_{R}
that, as discussed, is accessible if
Q_{L}
is fixed.

Finally, one can consider the thermodynamic potentials when
Q_{L,R}
are held fixed. The temperatures one obtains then are

\frac{1}{T_{L}^{\prime}}=\sqrt{\frac{\pi c}{12}}\frac{R}{\sqrt{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{L}}}=\frac{1}{T_{L}^{[0]}}\;,\hskip 20.20164pt\frac{1}{T_{R}^{\prime}}=\sqrt{\frac{\pi c}{12}}\bigg(\frac{R-\lambda Q_{L}+\lambda Q_{R}}{\sqrt{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{R}}}\bigg)=\frac{R_{v}}{RT_{R}^{[0]}}

(3.108)

In terms of these deformed temperatures, the entropy takes the form

S=\frac{\pi c}{6}(RT^{\prime}_{L}+R_{v}T^{\prime}_{R})

(3.109)

again, a minimal departure from Cardy’s formula, despite the fact that the theory is rather different. In particular, this departure would be invisible if one concentrates on states that had
J_{0}=\bar{J}_{0}
in the undeformed CFT.

Modified modular invariance

Consider the partition function of a
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFT on a torus with modular parameter
\tau
and length of the
a
-cycle
R

Z_{J\bar{T}}\left(\tau,\bar{\tau},\nu,\frac{\lambda}{R}\right)=\sum e^{-\tau_{2}RE_{n}^{[\lambda]}(R)+i\tau_{1}RP_{n}+2\pi i\nu q_{n}^{[\lambda]}}

(3.110)

where
\nu
is a chemical potential that couples to the chiral
U(1)
current. We explicitly indicated the fact that the partition function depends on the dimensionless ratio
\lambda/R

as an argument - rather than a label - because it changes under diffeomorphisms, due to its vectorial
nature.
Due to the presence of imaginary energy modes, it is currently not
well understood to what extent this partition function is well defined; however, the fact that the
theory admits a non-perturbative definition
[
135
]
yields hope that its study is meaningful.

The modular transformation properties of this partition function were first discussed in
[
146
]
. To derive them, it is useful to introduce the complex coordinates on the torus,
z=x+\tau y,\,\bar{z}=x+\bar{\tau}y
, in terms of which the metric takes the form
ds^{2}=R^{2}dzd\bar{z}
. Under the modular transformation (
3.32
),
z,\bar{z}
transform as

z\rightarrow\frac{z}{c\tau+d}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;\bar{z}\rightarrow\frac{\bar{z}}{c\bar{\tau}+d}

(3.111)

Since the
J\bar{T}
coupling
\lambda^{a}
transforms as a vector,
\lambda^{\bar{z}}
, under modular transformations, which has the same transformation
properties as
R\bar{z}
, it follows that the dimensionless combination
\lambda/R
transforms exactly as
\bar{z}
. A similar argument
can be used to derive the well-known transformation properties
30
30

30

The chemical potential is related to a background gauge field,
a^{z}
, that couples to the left current as
\nu=\beta a^{z}=\tau_{2}Ra^{z}
.
Invariance of the action under diffeomorphisms implies that
a^{z}
transforms in the opposite way from
R\bar{z}
, which leads to
Ra^{z}\rightarrow(c\bar{\tau}+d)Ra^{z}
. Taking into account the transformation of
\tau_{2}
, we find the quoted result.
of the chemical potential
\nu
. With this in mind, the partition function has the standard anomalous transformation under diffeomorphisms of the torus

Z\left(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d},\frac{a\bar{\tau}+b}{c\bar{\tau}+d},\frac{\nu}{c\tau+d},\frac{\lambda}{R(c\bar{\tau}+d)}\right)=e^{\frac{2i\pi kc\nu^{2}}{c\tau+d}}Z\left(\tau,\bar{\tau},\nu,\frac{\lambda}{R}\right)

(3.112)

Note the above transformation properties hold for
any
UV-complete two-dimensional QFT with a single (null) vector coupling with length dimension one, namely for any two-dimensional dipole CFT, as per our definition.

If, in addition to modular covariance - which is simply a statement about the well-definiteness of the torus partition function - one makes the additional, rather strong assumption that the energy spectrum of the deformed theory only depends on
\lambda
and the undeformed value of the respective level (taken to be generic), then one can again show
[
146
]
that the unique solution satisfying these assumptions (and the initial condition) is that of
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs. This partition function turns out to obey a flow equation with respect to
\lambda
, which can be in principle be reproduced using the non-perturbative definition of
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs. These considerations can be easily generalised to UV - complete QFTs whose dimensionful coupling is a vector that is not necessarily null.

The spectrum of conformal dimensions on the plane

As we have discussed, the spectrum of
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs on a cylinder exhibits imaginary energy states, which we have linked to the appearance of closed timelike curves (CTCs) in the theory in any finite size. Since these CTCs are absent in the planar limit, there is a logical possibility that
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs on the plane are inconsistency-free, following the suggestion of
[
147
]
.

In this subsubsection, we bring evidence that this is indeed the case, by computing the spectrum of
SL(2,\mathbb{R})_{L}
conformal dimensions of the theory on the plane - a natural observable, since
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs have left-moving conformal invariance. Given the structure of the
J\bar{T}
coupling, the general expectation
[
115
]
is that these dimensions depend on the right-moving momentum as
h(\lambda\bar{p})
.

These conformal dimensions can be obtained from known spectrum of left-moving energies on a cylinder via an infinite boost. The latter is necessary because the cylinder identification, unless it is null, breaks the left conformal symmetry. Let
x^{\pm}=\sigma\pm t
be the coordinates on the cylinder, originally identified as
x^{\pm}\sim x^{\pm}+R
. Under a boost

x^{\pm}\rightarrow\tilde{x}^{\pm}=e^{\mp\gamma}x^{\pm}

(3.113)

the identification radii of the tilded coordinates will be
\tilde{R}_{\pm}=Re^{\mp\gamma}
, while the
relation between energies and
J\bar{T}
coupling are

\lambda=\tilde{\lambda}\,e^{-\gamma}\;,\;\;\;\;\;E_{L}=e^{-\gamma}\tilde{E}_{L}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;E_{R}=e^{\gamma}\tilde{E}_{R}

(3.114)

as follows from their Lorentz transformation properties.

E_{L,R}
are the energies on the unboosted cylinder, given by
(
3.92
) - (
3.93
). We will be interested in the limit
\gamma\rightarrow\infty
in which
\tilde{R}_{-}
decompactifies, thus restoring the
SL(2,\mathbb{R})_{L}
symmetry. We would like the parameters
\tilde{\lambda}
,
\tilde{R}_{+}
and the right-moving energy
\tilde{E}_{R}
in the boosted theory to be fixed in this limit. Plugging (
3.114
) into the expression for
E_{L}
we find

\tilde{E}_{L}=e^{\gamma}\left(E_{R}+\frac{2\pi(h_{L}-h_{R})}{R}\right)=\frac{2\pi\left(h_{L}-c/24\right)}{\tilde{R}_{+}}+e^{2\gamma}\left(\tilde{E}_{R}-\frac{2\pi(h_{R}-c/24)\,e^{-2\gamma}}{\tilde{R}_{+}}\right)

(3.115)

The only way that
\tilde{E}_{L}
could be finite is if
\tilde{E}_{R}
cancels against
2\pi(h_{R}-c/24)\,e^{-2\gamma}/\tilde{R}_{+}
with precision
e^{-2\gamma}
. Since
\tilde{E}_{R}\equiv\bar{p}
itself is finite, we conclude that we should scale

h_{R}-\frac{c}{24}=\frac{\bar{p}\tilde{R}_{+}}{2\pi}\,e^{2\gamma}+a+\mathcal{O}(e^{-2\gamma})

(3.116)

where
a
is some
\mathcal{O}(1)
dimensionless quantity.
Plugging this expansion of
h_{R}
into the expression
(
3.92
) for
E_{R}
and expanding in the
\gamma\rightarrow\infty
limit, we find

\displaystyle\tilde{E}_{R}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4\pi e^{-\gamma}}{\tilde{\lambda}^{2}ke^{-2\gamma}}\left(\tilde{R}_{+}e^{\gamma}-\tilde{\lambda}\,q^{[0]}e^{-\gamma}-\sqrt{\left(\tilde{R}_{+}e^{\gamma}-\tilde{\lambda}\,q^{[0]}e^{-\gamma}\right)^{2}-\frac{\tilde{\lambda}^{2}k}{2\pi}\bar{p}\tilde{R}_{+}-\tilde{\lambda}^{2}kae^{-2\gamma}+\ldots}\right)

(3.117)

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\bar{p}+\frac{1}{\tilde{R}_{+}}\left(2\pi a+\tilde{\lambda}\bar{p}q^{[0]}+\frac{k\tilde{\lambda}^{2}\bar{p}^{2}}{8\pi}\right)\,e^{-2\gamma}+\mathcal{O}(e^{-4\gamma})

where we have relabeled
J_{0}\rightarrow q^{[0]}
.
Plugging this expansion back into (
3.115
) and taking
\gamma\rightarrow\infty
, we arrive at

\tilde{E}_{L}=\frac{2\pi}{\tilde{R}_{+}}\left(h_{L}-\frac{c}{24}+\frac{\tilde{\lambda}}{2\pi}\bar{p}q^{[0]}+\frac{k\tilde{\lambda}^{2}\bar{p}^{2}}{16\pi^{2}}\right)

(3.118)

the factors of
a
cancelling out.
Using the usual map from the cylinder to the plane, which maps energies to conformal dimensions, we find that the deformed
SL(2,\mathbb{R})_{L}
dimensions are

h^{[\lambda]}=h^{[0]}+\frac{\lambda}{2\pi}q^{[0]}\bar{p}+\frac{\lambda^{2}k}{16\pi^{2}}\bar{p}^{2}

(3.119)

where we have dropped the tilde from
\lambda
and rewrote the undeformed left dimension as
h_{L}\rightarrow h^{[0]}
. The expression for the charge in the deformed theory is given by (
3.91
), which in our new notation reads

q^{[\lambda]}=q^{[0]}+\frac{\lambda k}{4\pi}\bar{p}

(3.120)

The above expressions correspond to an exact formula for the conformal dimensions and charges in
J\bar{T}
-deformed CFTs as a function of the deformation parameter. As expected, these data only depend on the combination
\lambda\bar{p}
and, unlike the spectrum on the cylinder, show no obvious pathology as
\lambda
becomes large. Remarkably, these exact expressions terminate at
\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})
. The combination
\hat{h}\equiv h^{[\lambda]}-q_{[\lambda]}^{2}/k

is independent of
\lambda
, in perfect agreement with the argument presented in
[
142
]
,
and hints towards a possible interpretation of the
J\bar{T}
deformation as spectral flow.

A simple consequence of the above formulae is that
purely holomorphic quantities do not acquire an anomalous dimension, as they have
\bar{p}=0
; however, initially purely antiholomorphic quantities, such as the right-moving stress tensor, receives a non-trivial left-moving anomalous dimension and charge

h_{\bar{T}}^{[\lambda]}=\frac{\lambda^{2}k\bar{p}^{2}}{16\pi^{2}}\;,\;\;\;\;\;q_{\bar{T}}^{[\lambda]}=\frac{\lambda k\bar{p}}{4\pi}

(3.121)

These formulae can be checked to leading order(s) using conformal perturbation theory, finding perfect agreement
[
148
]
. The precise expression for the corresponding operator in terms of the fields in the theory is however not entirely clear.

II. Classical
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs: Lagrangians and Hamiltonians

So far, we have discussed quantum properties of
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs, such as their spectrum. In this subsection, we turn to studying in detail the classical action and Hamiltonian of certain
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs, paying special attention to the conserved currents that exist in the theory. These will be essential for understanding the holographic dictionary in section
4.3
and the extended symmetries in section
5
. We start with the
J\bar{T}
- deformed free boson, and then discuss general
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs in the Hamiltonian formalism.

The
J\bar{T}
- deformed free boson

The
J\bar{T}
- deformed free boson was first studied in
[
132
]
. The
SL(2,\mathbb{R})_{L}\times U(1)_{R}
symmetry of the problem require the action to take the form

S_{J\bar{T}}=-\int dUdV\partial_{U}\phi\partial_{V}\phi\,\mathcal{F}(\lambda\partial_{V}\phi)

(3.122)

for some function
\mathcal{F}
satisfying
\mathcal{F}(0)=1
. The
J\bar{T}
flow equation (
3.83
), where the
U(1)
current is e.g. taken to be the shift symmetry current
31
31

31

It turns out one obtains the same result for the deformed Lagrangian if one uses instead the topological current (
3.129
), or the chiral one (
3.132
).
, determines it to be

\mathcal{F}(x)=\frac{1}{1-x}

(3.123)

The equations of motion are simply

\partial_{V}\left(\frac{\partial_{U}\phi}{1-\lambda\partial_{V}\phi}\right)=0

(3.124)

As in the
T\bar{T}
case, there exists a field-dependent coordinate transformation
32
32

32

In this section, as well as in the next one, we will be abusively using the same notation
u,v
for the field-dependent coordinates, even though their expression in terms of the fields basic changes from (
3.46
) in
T\bar{T}
, to (
3.125
) in
J\bar{T}
. We hope that this choice, made for ease of notation, will not cause confusion to the reader.

u=U\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;v=V-\lambda\phi(U,V)+const.

(3.125)

that maps the system to a free boson, this time already at the level of the action.
Indeed, using the relation between the first derivatives of
\phi

\partial_{U}\phi=\frac{\partial_{u}\phi}{1+\lambda\partial_{v}\phi}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;\partial_{V}\phi=\frac{\partial_{v}\phi}{1+\lambda\partial_{v}\phi}

(3.126)

one can easily check that the action (
3.122
) becomes
\int dudv\,\partial_{u}\phi\partial_{v}\phi
.

Let us now discuss the conserved currents in this theory. Using the Noether procedure, one can compute the components of the stress tensor, finding

T_{UU}=\frac{(\partial_{U}\phi)^{2}}{(1-\lambda\partial_{V}\phi)^{2}}\;,\;\;\;\;\;T_{VU}=0\;,\;\;\;\;\;T_{UV}=\frac{\lambda\partial_{U}\phi(\partial_{V}\phi)^{2}}{(1-\lambda\partial_{V}\phi)^{2}}\;,\;\;\;\;\;T_{VV}=\frac{(\partial_{V}\phi)^{2}}{1-\lambda\partial_{V}\phi}

(3.127)

The stress tensor is not symmetric because the
J\bar{T}
deformation breaks Lorentz invariance. The
T_{VU}
component vanishes as a consequence of the
SL(2,\mathbb{R})
symmetry preserved by the deformation.

The
U(1)
Noether current associated to the shift symmetry of
\phi
is

J_{\mbox{\tiny{$U$}}}^{sh}=\frac{\partial_{U}\phi}{(1-\lambda\partial_{V}\phi)^{2}}\;,\;\;\;\;\;J_{\mbox{\tiny{$V$}}}^{sh}=\frac{\partial_{V}\phi}{1-\lambda\partial_{V}\phi}

(3.128)

There is, additionally, a topologically conserved current,
J^{top}=\star d\phi
, with components

J_{{\mbox{\tiny{$U$}}}}^{top}=\partial_{U}\phi\;,\;\;\;\;\;J_{{\mbox{\tiny{$V$}}}}^{top}=-\partial_{V}\phi

(3.129)

The conserved charges associated to the two currents will be denoted as

Q_{0}=\int d\sigma(J^{sh}_{\mbox{\tiny{$U$}}}-J_{\mbox{\tiny{$V$}}}^{sh})\equiv J_{0}+\bar{J}_{0}\;,\;\;\;\;\;w=\int d\sigma(J^{top}_{\mbox{\tiny{$U$}}}-J_{\mbox{\tiny{$V$}}}^{top})\equiv J_{0}-\bar{J}_{0}

(3.130)

Both will be assumed to be quantized.
Note that in a CFT, the combinations
J^{\pm}=\frac{1}{2}(J^{sh}\pm J^{top})
are chiral and, respectively, anti-chiral, with charges denoted as
J_{0},\bar{J}_{0}
, as in the previous subsection.

Due to the
SL(2,\mathbb{R})_{L}
symmetry of the deformed theory, we would still expect
[
93
]
to be able to construct a purely left-moving conserved current. Indeed, it is not hard to notice that the combination

K_{\alpha}\equiv\frac{1}{2}(J^{sh}_{\alpha}+J^{top}_{\alpha}-\lambda T_{\alpha V})

(3.131)

has a vanishing right-moving component. Concretely,

K_{U}=\frac{\partial_{U}\phi}{1-\lambda\partial_{V}\phi}\;,\;\;\;\;\;K_{V}=0

(3.132)

The left-moving component is holomorphically conserved, as seen by using the equations of motion (
3.124
). We also note that
T_{\mbox{\tiny{$UU$}}}=K_{\mbox{\tiny{$U$}}}^{2}
, so the combination
T_{\mbox{\tiny{$UU$}}}-K_{\mbox{\tiny{$U$}}}^{2}
is trivially independent of
\lambda
. As we will see, the
\lambda
- independence of this combination generalizes and is related to the fact that the
J\bar{T}
deformation induces an operator-valued spectral flow transformation on the left-movers
[
142
,
148
]
.

The conserved charge associated to the chiral current
K_{U}
will be denoted
33
33

33

In our conventions,
k=2\pi
for a free boson, explaining the difference with (
3.91
). We will sometimes use
\hat{k}=k/(2\pi)
to ease the notation.

Q_{L}=\int d\sigma K_{U}=J_{0}+\frac{\lambda}{2}E_{R}

(3.133)

One can also construct an analogous ‘right-moving’ current
34
34

34

This terminology should not be taken literally, as the on-shell solution for
\bar{K}_{\alpha}
is is not just a function of
V
; in the case of the stress tensor,
by ‘right-moving’ we simply mean the generator of translations in the right-moving null direction V.

\bar{K}_{\alpha}=\frac{1}{2}(J^{sh}_{\alpha}-J^{top}_{\alpha}-\lambda T_{\alpha V})\;,\;\;\;\mbox{or}\;\;\;\;\;\;\bar{K}_{U}=\frac{\lambda\partial_{U}\phi\partial_{V}\phi}{1-\lambda\partial_{V}\phi}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\bar{K}_{V}=\partial_{V}\phi

(3.134)

The associated conserved charge will be denoted as

Q_{R}=\int d\sigma(\bar{K}_{U}-\bar{K}_{V})=\bar{J}_{0}+\frac{\lambda}{2}E_{R}

(3.135)

We note that the difference
Q_{L}-Q_{R}=J_{0}-\bar{J}_{0}=w
is
\lambda
- independent.

Since the currents
T_{UU}
and
K_{U}
are both chiral and conserved, it follows that they will still be so if we multiply them by arbitrary functions of
U
, thus obtaining an infinite set of conserved currents, one for each Fourier mode of these functions. As far as the right-moving currents are concerned, it is not hard to note that

\frac{T_{UV}}{T_{VV}}=\frac{\bar{K}_{U}}{\bar{K}_{V}}=\frac{\lambda\partial_{U}\phi}{1-\lambda\partial_{V}\phi}=-\frac{\partial_{U}v}{\partial_{V}v}

(3.136)

where
v
is the field-dependent coordinate introduced in (
3.125
). This implies that the currents
\bar{f}(v)T_{\alpha V}
and

\bar{\chi}(v)\bar{K}_{\alpha}
are conserved for any functions
\bar{f}(v),\bar{\chi}(v)
, as is any linear combination of
J^{-}_{\alpha}
and
T_{\alpha V}
, when multiplied by an arbitrary function of
v
. This observation will be very important in building an infinite set of conserved charges in this theory.

Classical Hamiltonian of general
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs

We can easily compute the Hamiltonian of the
J\bar{T}
- deformed free boson. However, it is possible to have the answer for a general seed Hamiltonian, under certain assumptions about the initial CFT.

We consider a two-dimensional classical field theory with fields
\phi_{k}
, with
k=1,\ldots,n
. We assume that the field theory has a
U(1)
symmetry, which for simplicity will be taken to be a shift symmetry of the first scalar field,
\phi_{1}
. The Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}(\pi_{k},\phi_{k})
thus only depends on
\phi_{1}
through its spatial derivatives. The
U(1)
shift current has components

J^{sh}_{t}=\pi_{1}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;J^{sh}_{\sigma}=\partial_{\phi_{1}^{\prime}}\mathcal{H}

(3.137)

The components of the topologically conserved current (
3.129
) are

J^{top}_{t}=\phi_{1}^{\prime}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;J^{top}_{\sigma}=\partial_{\pi_{1}}\mathcal{H}

(3.138)

It is convenient to introduce the following combinations
of the time components of these two currents

\mathcal{J}_{\pm}=\frac{\pi_{1}\pm\phi_{1}^{\prime}}{2}

(3.139)

Of course, this only leads to a
U(1)
current algebra with (rescaled) level
\hat{k}=1
; nonetheless,
the level can be easily set to any desired value by rescaling
\mathcal{J}_{\pm}
by
\hat{k}^{1/2}
, which we will view
as a corresponding rescaling of
\phi_{1},\pi_{1}
.
We will furthermore assume that in the undeformed CFT, the currents
J^{\pm}\equiv\frac{1}{2}(J^{sh}\pm J^{top})
are chiral and, respectively, anti-chiral, which will impose certain constraints on the undeformed Hamiltonian density
\mathcal{H}^{[0]}
. More precisely, the vanishing of their (anti)chiral components requires that
\mathcal{H}_{R}^{[0]}=\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{H}^{[0]}-\mathcal{P})
satisfy

J^{+}_{{{\mbox{\tiny{$V$}}}}}=\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{\pi_{1}}+\partial_{\phi^{\prime}_{1}})\mathcal{H}_{R}^{[0]}=0

(3.140)

and

J^{-}_{{\mbox{\tiny{$U$}}}}=\frac{1}{2}(\pi_{1}-\phi^{\prime}_{1})-\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{\pi_{1}}-\partial_{\phi^{\prime}_{1}})\mathcal{H}_{R}^{[0]}=0

(3.141)

which ultimately imply that
\mathcal{H}^{[0]}
has a decoupled free boson subsector,
\mathcal{H}^{[0]}=\frac{1}{2}(\pi_{1}^{2}+\phi_{1}^{\prime 2})+\mathcal{H}^{[0]}(other)
. One also assumes that the undeformed Hamiltonian has dimension
2
.

We first consider deforming the Hamiltonian by the
J^{sh}\bar{T}
operator constructed from the above current and the components of the stress tensor which, in terms of the deformed Hamiltonian density, are given by (
3.52
). The flow equation for the Hamiltonian density is

\partial_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}=-\mathcal{O}_{J^{sh}\bar{T}}=J^{sh}_{t}T_{\sigma V}-J^{sh}_{\sigma}T_{tV}

(3.142)

In terms of the right-moving energy density
\mathcal{H}_{R}=\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{P})
, the expression for
J^{sh}\bar{T}
simplifies to

\mathcal{O}_{J\bar{T}}=2(\pi_{1}\partial_{\pi_{k}}\mathcal{H}_{R}\partial_{\phi^{\prime}_{k}}\mathcal{H}_{R}-\mathcal{H}_{R}\,\partial_{\phi^{\prime}_{1}}\mathcal{H}_{R})

(3.143)

resulting in a flow equation for
\mathcal{H}_{R}
only.
One can then use this explicit expression to show that, at least in the single-field case, the
VU
component of the stress tensor will stay zero along the flow, if it starts out being zero. This implies that the
J\bar{T}
flow preserves the initial
SL(2,\mathbb{R})_{L}
symmetry, as expected
[
179
]
.

The authors of
[
179
]
also showed that, if one assumes that
\mathcal{H}_{R}
in the deformed theory is a function of only
\lambda^{2}\mathcal{H}_{R}^{[0]}
and
\lambda\mathcal{J}_{+}

- an Ansatz motivated by dimensional analysis and the universality of the deformation - then the right-moving Hamiltonian density that solves the
J\bar{T}
flow equation is given by

\mathcal{H}_{R}=\frac{2}{\lambda^{2}\hat{k}}\left(1-\lambda\mathcal{J}_{+}-\sqrt{(1-\lambda\mathcal{J}_{+})^{2}-\lambda^{2}\hat{k}\mathcal{H}_{R}^{[0]}}\right)

(3.144)

where we have reinstated an arbitrary level by rescaling
\lambda\rightarrow\lambda\sqrt{\hat{k}}
and
\mathcal{J}_{+}\rightarrow\mathcal{J}_{+}/\sqrt{\hat{k}}
in the
\hat{k}=1
result, while replacing
\mathcal{H}_{R}^{[0]}
by the corresponding Hamiltonian density in the theory with level
\hat{k}
.

Note that, as in the case of
T\bar{T}
, this has the same functional form as the deformed energy formula (
3.92
), but applied at the level of the currents, rather than that of the global conserved charges. Since (
3.144
) is symmetric in
\pi_{1}\leftrightarrow\phi_{1}^{\prime}
, the
J\bar{T}
deformation defined using
J^{sh}
leads to the same (classical) deformed theory as the one defined using
J^{top}
