description, case in which the correct description of the bulk is only via the mixed boundary conditions, and ii) defining the theory via the bulk with a sharp radial cutoff, case in which additional double-trace operators corresponding to matter fields must be added to the boundary action
[
202
]
. Unlike the SZ definition of
T\bar{T}
, this procedure is only defined at leading order in
1/N
, by effectively transposing the gravitational picture in field-theoretical language, and there is no reason to expect that the theory resulting from these combined irrelevant deformations is UV - complete.

Despite of the above issues, the relation between a sharp radial cutoff and
T\bar{T}
has found many applications and generalisations, such as to higher dimensions
[
203
,
204
]
(where the higher-dimensional analogue to
T\bar{T}
is defined by requiring Dirichlet boundary conditions for gravity in AdS
d>3
at a fixed finite radius), to de Sitter spacetimes
[
205
]
(where, in addition to cutting off the energy spectrum, one further modifies the energies via a
\mu
- dependent cosmological constant ), to tensor networks
[
206
]
and attempts to build holographic examples with emergent time
[
207
]
. In all of these cases, the ‘
T\bar{T}
- deformed CFT’ is defined via the bulk at large
N
and does not possess an independent boundary definition;
in a certain sense, it is just a new name given to the gravitational path integral with a finite radial cutoff. It would of course be valuable if one could put the boundary theory on a firmer footing, in view of all these potentially interesting applications.
For further discussion of these points, see
[
149
,
208
]
.

4.3
The holographic dictionary for
J\bar{T}
-deformed CFTs

In this subsection, we apply the exactly the same series of steps to derive the holographic dictionary for
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs. The first version of this holographic dictionary, which holds when
J
is a chiral current, was proposed in
[
178
]
. We will instead follow the more recent version of this dictionary presented in
[
144
]
, in which the
U(1)
current is non-chiral, and the analysis of anomalies is clarified.

Step 1: the relation between the deformed and undeformed sources and vevs

The minimal set of phase space variables that we need to consider are the stress tensor
T^{\alpha}{}_{a}
and current
J^{\alpha}
, which are canonically conjugate to the boundary vielbein
e_{\alpha}{}^{a}
and gauge field,
\mathrm{a}_{\alpha}
. Here, latin indices denote the tangent space and greek ones are spacetime indices. The reason we prefer the vielbein formulation is that the deformed theory is not Lorentz invariant; consequently, the conserved stress tensor is not symmetric and it naturally couples to the vielbein, and not the metric. The variation of the on-shell action (identified with the classical limit of the deformed generating functional) reads

\delta S=\int d^{2}x\,e\,\left(T^{\alpha}{}_{a}\,\delta e_{\alpha}^{a}+J^{\alpha}\delta\mathrm{a}_{\alpha}\right)

(4.36)

where
e=\det e^{a}_{\alpha}
and all quantities are in the deformed theories at finite
\lambda
and should, in principle, carry this label that we momentarily omit.

The
J\bar{T}
coupling is naturally a (null) vector,
\lambda^{a}
, in tangent space
6
6

6

The manipulations to follow apply equally well to
JT^{a}
- deformed QFTs, for which
\lambda^{a}
need not be null.
. We will write
\lambda^{a}=\lambda\hat{\lambda}^{a}
, where
\hat{\lambda}^{a}
is a unit
tangent space vector or its null counterpart.
The variation of the Lorentzian on-shell action when
\lambda
is infinitesimally changed is
given by minus the double-trace deformation of the boundary action

\partial_{\lambda^{a}}S^{[\lambda^{a}]}=-\int d^{2}x\,e\,\mathcal{O}_{J\bar{T}}^{[\lambda^{a}]}=-\hat{\lambda}^{a}\int d^{2}x\,e\,\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}J^{\alpha}T^{\beta}{}_{a}

(4.37)

where the
JT^{a}
operator has been appropriately covariantized.

In order to ensure the deformation is well-defined also in presence of external sources, we
assume the current
J^{\alpha}
is exactly conserved, i.e. it is not anomalous. The requirement that

S^{[\lambda^{a}+\Delta\lambda^{a}]}
have a good variational principle translates into being able to write

\delta S^{\lambda^{a}+\Delta\lambda^{a}}=\delta S^{\lambda^{a}}-\Delta\lambda^{a}\int d^{2}x\,\delta\left(e\,\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}J^{\alpha}T^{\beta}{}_{a}\right)

(4.38)

again in the form (
4.36
), for some slightly modified canonical variables (the variation
\Delta\lambda^{a}
is infinitesimal). Plugging in, we have

\displaystyle\delta S^{[\lambda^{a}+\Delta\lambda^{a}]}\!\!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\!\int d^{2}x\left[e\,T^{\alpha}{}_{a}(\delta e_{\alpha}{}^{a}+\Delta\lambda^{a}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\delta J^{\beta})+eJ^{\alpha}(\delta\mathrm{a}_{\alpha}-\Delta\lambda^{a}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\delta T^{\beta}{}_{a})+\Delta\lambda^{a}T^{\alpha}{}_{a}\delta(e\epsilon_{\alpha\beta})J^{\beta}\right]

(4.39)

\displaystyle\hskip-76.82234pt=\int d^{2}x\left[e\,T^{\alpha}{}_{a}\delta(e_{\alpha}{}^{a}+\Delta\lambda^{a}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}J^{\beta})+eJ^{\alpha}\delta(\mathrm{a}_{\alpha}-\Delta\lambda^{a}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}T^{\beta}{}_{a})+\Delta\lambda^{a}T^{\alpha}{}_{a}\left(\delta(e\epsilon_{\alpha\beta})-2e\delta\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\right)J^{\beta}\right]

Using
\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}=e\hat{\epsilon}_{\alpha\beta}
, where
\hat{\epsilon}_{\alpha\beta}
is the Levi-Civita symbol, one can show the term in the last paranthesis vanishes, and thus the variation of the action
\delta S^{[\lambda^{a}+\Delta\lambda^{a}]}
again takes the form (
4.36
), with slightly modified sources and vevs. From (
4.39
), we can easily read off the change in these data with the flow parameter
\lambda
, defined as above
7
7

7

These solutions are rather similar to those obtained in
[
178
]
, if one particularises
\lambda^{a}
to be a null vector, additionally assumes that
J^{\alpha}=\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}J_{\beta}
, and considers a coupling of the current to
\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}\mathrm{a}_{\beta}
instead. See
[
178
,
144
]
for more details.

\partial_{\lambda}e_{\alpha}{}^{a}=\hat{\lambda}^{a}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}J^{\beta}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\partial_{\lambda}\mathrm{a}_{\alpha}=-\hat{\lambda}^{a}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}T^{\beta}{}_{a}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\partial_{\lambda}(eJ^{\alpha})=\partial_{\lambda}(eT^{\alpha}{}_{a})=0

(4.40)

These flow equations are trivially solved by

e^{[\lambda]\,a}_{\alpha}=e_{\alpha}^{[0]\,a}+\lambda^{a}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}J^{\beta}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\mathrm{a}_{\alpha}^{[\lambda]}=\mathrm{a}_{\alpha}^{[0]}-\lambda^{a}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}T^{\beta}{}_{a}

(4.41)

(e\,J^{\alpha})^{[\lambda]}=(e\,J^{\alpha})^{[0]}\;,\;\;\;\;\;(e\,T^{\alpha}{}_{a})^{[\lambda]}=(e\,T^{\alpha}{}_{a})^{[0]}

(4.42)

The expectation values that shift the sources in (
4.41
) can be considered in either the undeformed or deformed theory, as implied by (
4.42
).

Step 2: the holographic dictionary

In order to write down the holographic dictionary, one first needs to correctly model the
U(1)
currents, and in particular their anomalies, in the undeformed CFT. We assume that the current
J
that enters the definition of the
J\bar{T}
operator is not anomalous, and thus we can globally write

J^{\alpha}=\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\phi

(4.43)

for some scalar field
\phi
, defined via the above bosonisation of the current
J
. While this definition holds in principle in the deformed theory, the map (
4.42
) implies it it will be true also in the undeformed CFT to which we map, where the vielbein is
e^{[0]}
. Note that for almost all this subsection we will be working in the undeformed CFT, though we will mostly omit the corresponding labels
[0]
.

The Hodge dual
J^{\prime}=\star J
, which is analogous to the usual shift current, is then anomalous

\nabla_{\alpha}J^{\prime[0]\alpha}=-\frac{k}{2\pi}\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}_{[0]}\partial_{\alpha}\mathrm{a}_{\beta}^{[0]}

(4.44)

These properties of the two currents can be easily modeled in holography using two three-dimensional Chern-Simons gauge fields
[
209
]
, whose (bulk) anomaly reproduces the field theory one

S_{CS}=-\frac{k}{8\pi}\int d^{3}x\sqrt{g}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}(A_{\mu}+B_{\mu})\partial_{\nu}(A_{\rho}-B_{\rho})

(4.45)

This action is by construction invariant under equal and opposite gauge transformations of
A,B
, but picks up a boundary term under equal shifts of the gauge potential; thus, only half of the bulk gauge symmetry is preserved at the boundary. The perhaps unusual minus sign in front of the action is due to our unusual conventions for the
\epsilon
tensor. The Chern-Simons equations of motion imply as usual that the gauge connections are flat; choosing the radial gauge
A_{\rho}=B_{\rho}=0
moreover implies that the remaining components of the bulk fields,
A_{\alpha},B_{\alpha}
are independent of
\rho
, and thus effectively two-dimensional.

As is well-known, the action (
4.45
) does not have a well-defined variational principle. To make it so, we need to add a boundary term
[
210
]
. A choice that yields - in Minkowski space - a chiral current coupling to
A_{-}
and an anti-chiral one coupling to
B_{+}
is

\displaystyle S_{bnd}

\displaystyle=\frac{k}{16\pi}\int_{\partial}d^{2}x\sqrt{-\gamma}\gamma^{\alpha\beta}(A+B)_{\alpha}(A+B)_{\beta}

(4.46)

Only this term will contribute to the stress tensor, since (
4.45
) is topological. The variation of the action is:

\displaystyle\delta S

\displaystyle=\delta S_{CS}+\delta S_{bnd}=-\frac{k}{8\pi}\int d^{3}x\sqrt{-g}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}(F^{A}_{\mu\nu}\delta A_{\rho}-F^{B}_{\mu\nu}\delta B_{\rho})+

\displaystyle+\frac{k}{8\pi}\int_{\partial}d^{2}x\sqrt{-\gamma}\bigg[(A_{\alpha}+B_{\alpha})(\gamma^{\alpha\beta}-\epsilon^{\alpha\beta})\delta A_{\beta}+(A_{\alpha}+B_{\alpha})(\gamma^{\alpha\beta}+\epsilon^{\alpha\beta})\delta B_{\beta}\bigg]+

\displaystyle+\frac{k}{16\pi}\int_{\partial}d^{2}x\sqrt{-\gamma}\bigg[(A+B)_{\alpha}(A+B)_{\beta}-\frac{\gamma_{\alpha\beta}}{2}\gamma^{\mu\nu}(A+B)_{\mu}(A+B)_{\nu}\bigg]\delta\gamma^{\alpha\beta}

(4.47)

where we have taken into account the fact that the outward-pointing unit normal to the AdS boundary is
-(2\rho/\ell)\,\partial_{\rho}
. From the second line above, we immediately read off the components of the two chiral currents, which are linear combinations of the form
\frac{1}{2}(\star J\pm J)
.
It is then natural to identify the topological current (
4.43
) as

J^{[0]\alpha}=\frac{k}{4\pi}\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}(A_{\beta}+B_{\beta})

(4.48)

since the right-hand side is conserved, using the Chern-Simons equations of motion, for
any
flat connections
A,B
, just like the left-hand side is conserved with no restriction on
\phi
. This identification implies of course that

J^{\prime[0]}_{\alpha}=\frac{k}{4\pi}(A_{\alpha}+B_{\alpha})

(4.49)

To identify the field theory source that couples to the topological current (
4.48
), we simply interpret the second line in the action variation (
4.3
) as
\int d^{2}x\sqrt{-\gamma}J^{\alpha}\delta\mathrm{a}_{\alpha}
, with
J^{\alpha}
given in (
4.48
), plus some extra contribution proportional to the variation of the projectors. Concretely, on-shell the action variation (
4.3
) becomes

\displaystyle\delta S^{on-shell}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\partial}d^{2}x\sqrt{-\gamma}\,J^{[0]\alpha}\delta[A_{\alpha}-B_{\alpha}-\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}(A^{\beta}+B^{\beta})]-

(4.50)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-\frac{k}{16\pi}\int_{\partial}d^{2}x\sqrt{-\gamma}\bigg[(A+B)_{\alpha}(A+B)_{\beta}-\frac{\gamma_{\alpha\beta}}{2}(A+B)^{2}\bigg]\delta\gamma^{\alpha\beta}

This immediately leads to the identification

\mathrm{a}_{\alpha}=\frac{1}{2}[A_{\alpha}-B_{\alpha}-\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}(A^{\beta}+B^{\beta})]

(4.51)

The variation of the action also allows us to read off the Chern-Simons contribution to the energy-momentum tensor
8
8

8

Note that a naïve computation using just the last term in (
4.3
) would yield
minus
the above answer,
as
T_{\alpha\beta}=-\frac{2}{\sqrt{-\gamma}}\frac{\delta S}{\delta\gamma^{\alpha\beta}}
in Lorentzian signature. The contribution of the
\epsilon
tensor variation is
essential
for obtaining the correct relative sign between the stress tensor and the
U(1)
charge, to which the
J\bar{T}
-deformed spectrum is highly sensitive.

\displaystyle T^{CS}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{k}{8\pi}\bigg[(A_{\alpha}+B_{\alpha})(A_{\beta}+B_{\beta})-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{\alpha\beta}(A+B)^{2}\bigg]

(4.52)

Note that, since it only depends on the sum
A+B
, the Chern-Simons contribution to the stress tensor is also fully determined by
\phi
, i.e. it only involves the expectation values of the currents. The full stress tensor is given by the sum of the gravitational and the Chern-Simons contributions

T_{\alpha\beta}=T_{\alpha\beta}^{g}+T_{\alpha\beta}^{CS}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;\;T_{\alpha\beta}^{g}=\frac{1}{8\pi G\ell}g^{(2)}_{\alpha\beta}

(4.53)

where
g^{(2)}
is the first subleading Fefferman-Graham coefficient in (
4.18
), and we have assumed that the boundary metric
g^{(0)}\leftrightarrow\gamma^{[0]}
is flat, in order to drop an additional potential contribution to
T^{g}
proportional to its Ricci scalar.

To summarize, in terms of bulk fields, the undeformed sources
\gamma^{[0]}
and
\mathrm{a}_{\alpha}^{[0]}
correspond to
g^{(0)}
in the Fefferman-Graham expansion and, respectively, the combination (
4.51
) of the bulk gauge fields in radial gauge. The expectation values of the dual currents are encoded in (
4.48
) and the above expression for the holographic stress tensor. The bulk field combinations that correspond to the deformed sources and vevs are obtained by simply plugging this undeformed dictionary into (
4.41
) - (
4.42
).

The gravitational phase space

To build the gravitational phase space corresponding to the deformed theory, we set the sources in the deformed theory to zero and look for the most general solution to the bulk equations of motion satisfying the corresponding boundary conditions. Using (
4.41
), the latter read

e_{\alpha}^{[0]\,a}=\delta_{\alpha}^{a}-\lambda^{a}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}J^{\beta}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\mathrm{a}_{\alpha}^{[0]}=\lambda^{a}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}T^{\beta}{}_{a}

(4.54)

Plugging in (
4.43
), the CFT vielbein (
4.54
) then takes the form

e_{\alpha}^{[0]\,a}=\delta_{\alpha}^{a}-\lambda^{a}\partial_{\alpha}\phi=\begin{pmatrix}1&-\lambda\partial_{U}\phi\\
0&1-\lambda\partial_{V}\phi\end{pmatrix}

(4.55)

The most general solution to three-dimensional Einstein gravity with this boundary condition is simply obtained by acting with the diffeomorphism

u\rightarrow U\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;v\rightarrow V-\lambda\phi+const

(4.56)

on the most general Bañados metric, which is parametrised by two arbitrary functions
\mathcal{L}(u),\bar{\mathcal{L}}(v)
that are assumed to be given.

Let us now determine the bulk gauge fields, in radial gauge. Using the relation (
4.42
) between the expectation value of
J
in the deformed and the undeformed theory, we find that the current in the undeformed CFT on the background (
4.55
) is

J^{\alpha}_{[0]}=\frac{e^{[\lambda]}}{e^{[0]}}J^{\alpha}_{[\lambda]}=\left(-\frac{2\partial_{V}\phi}{1-\lambda\partial_{V}\phi},\frac{2\partial_{U}\phi}{1-\lambda\partial_{V}\phi}\right)\;\;\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\;\;J^{\prime[0]}_{\alpha}=\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}^{[0]}J^{[0]\,\beta}=(\partial_{U}\phi,\partial_{V}\phi)

(4.57)

Using the Chern-Simons holographic dictionary (
4.49
), this determines
A_{\alpha}+B_{\alpha}=\frac{4\pi}{k}\partial_{\alpha}\phi
. To determine the individual
A
and
B
we use holographic expression (
4.51
) for
\mathrm{a}_{\alpha}
and the relation (
4.54
), whose right-hand-side is known because the stress tensor only depends on
A+B
. The expressions simplify if we work in terms of the tangent space components
a=\pm
. For the stress tensor, we find

T_{++}=\frac{\mathcal{L}}{8\pi G\ell}+\frac{2\pi}{k}\frac{(\partial_{U}\phi)^{2}}{(1-\lambda\partial_{V}\phi)^{2}}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;T_{--}=\frac{\bar{\mathcal{L}}}{8\pi G\ell\,}+\frac{2\pi}{k}\frac{(\partial_{V}\phi)^{2}}{(1-\lambda\partial_{V}\phi)^{2}}

(4.58)

and
T_{+-}=T_{-+}=0
. For the gauge fields

\mathrm{a}_{+}=-B_{+}=0\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;\mathrm{a}_{-}=A_{-}=-\lambda T_{--}

(4.59)

The remaining components,
A_{+}
,
B_{-}
, are determined from knowledge of
A+B
in terms of the presumably known functions
\bar{\mathcal{L}},\phi
. The spacetime components of the holographic stress tensor and gauge fields can be obtained by simply multiplying with
e^{[0]\,a}_{\alpha}
, given in (
4.15
). See
[
144
]
for the complete expressions.

Note that so far in our holographic analysis, the field
\phi
could be arbitrary. Nonetheless,
the flatness conditions for the gauge fields result in
an equation of motion for it.
Even though there are two gauge fields, one obtains a single condition on
\phi
since, as we already discussed, the flatness condition for
A+B
does not yield a constraint. The flatness condition for
A-B
can be written, using
(
4.51
), as

\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}(A_{\beta}-B_{\beta})=2\nabla_{\alpha}(\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}\mathrm{a}_{\beta})+\nabla_{\alpha}(A^{\alpha}+B^{\alpha})=2\lambda^{a}\nabla_{\alpha}T^{\alpha}{}_{a}+\frac{4\pi}{k}\Box\phi=0

(4.60)

Since the stress tensor is conserved, the first term may be dropped, and we simply find that
\phi
satisfies the free wave equation in the non-trivial metric (
4.15
). The latter takes precisely the form of the equation of motion for a
J\bar{T}
-deformed free boson

\displaystyle\partial_{V}\left(\frac{\partial_{U}\phi}{1-\lambda\partial_{V}\phi}\right)=0

(4.61)

whose general solution takes the form

\phi(U,V)=f(U)+g(v)\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;v=V-\lambda\phi+const

(4.62)

It immediately follows that the quantities

\frac{\partial_{U}\phi}{1-\lambda\partial_{V}\phi}=f^{\prime}\equiv\mathcal{J}(U)\;,\;\;\;\;\;\frac{\partial_{V}\phi}{1-\lambda\partial_{V}\phi}=g^{\prime}\equiv\bar{\mathcal{J}}(v)

(4.63)

are only functions of
U
and, respectively, the field-dependent coordinate
v
. Note these quantities correspond precisely to the tangent space components of the current combinations
\frac{J^{\prime}\pm J}{2}
, which should be identified
9
9

9

To obtain the components of
K_{\alpha},\bar{K}_{\alpha}
in the deformed theory, one should first compute the components of
\frac{1}{2}(J^{\prime[0]}\pm J^{[0]})^{\alpha}
by multiplying with
e^{[0]\alpha}_{a}
, and then translate the currents to the deformed theory by multiplying by
e^{[0]}/e^{[\lambda]}
.
with
K_{\alpha},\bar{K}_{\alpha}
(
3.132
) - (
3.134
) in the
J\bar{T}
- deformed free boson case, and the more general currents (
3.145
) discussed in Hamiltonian language.

As the most general
J\bar{T}
- deformed background metric can be obtained by applying the diffeomorphism (
4.56
) to a general Bañados geometry, the general solution for the gauge fields can also be obtained from that for Chern-Simons fields with standard boundary conditions

A_{u}=\frac{4\pi}{k}\partial_{u}\phi\;,\;\;\;\;\;A_{v}=0\;,\;\;\;\;\;B_{u}=0\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;B_{v}=\frac{4\pi}{k}\partial_{v}\phi

(4.64)

via the same diffeomorphism, together with a field dependent gauge transformation

A_{\alpha}\rightarrow A_{\alpha}-\partial_{\alpha}\Lambda\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;B_{\alpha}\rightarrow B_{\alpha}+\partial_{\alpha}\Lambda\;,\;\;\;\;\;\Lambda=\lambda\int^{v}dv^{\prime}T_{--}(v^{\prime})

(4.65)

where
T_{--}
is given in (
4.58
) and is purely a function of
v
.

To summarize, the most general solution to the gravity - Chern-Simons equations of
motion that satisfies the
J\bar{T}
- deformed boundary conditions (
4.54
) is parametrized by
four arbitrary functions
\mathcal{L}(U),\bar{\mathcal{L}}(v),\mathcal{J}(U),\bar{\mathcal{J}}(v)
of the left-moving coordinate
U
and, respectively, of the
field-dependent ‘right-moving’ coordinate
v
. The latter is itself determined by
\mathcal{J},\bar{\mathcal{J}}
via the defining equation (
4.96
), with solution (
4.97
).
The locally AdS
3
metric and flat gauge fields are explicitly given by

\displaystyle ds^{2}

\displaystyle=\ell^{2}\frac{d\rho^{2}}{4\rho^{2}}+\bigg(\mathcal{L}+\frac{\lambda^{2}\bar{\mathcal{L}}\mathcal{J}^{2}}{(1+\lambda\bar{\mathcal{J}})^{2}}-\frac{1+\rho^{2}\mathcal{L}\bar{\mathcal{L}}}{\rho}\frac{\lambda\mathcal{J}}{1+\lambda\bar{\mathcal{J}}}\bigg)dU^{2}+\bigg(\frac{1+\rho^{2}\mathcal{L}\bar{\mathcal{L}}}{\rho(1+\lambda\bar{\mathcal{J}})}-\frac{2\lambda\bar{\mathcal{L}}\mathcal{J}}{(1+\lambda\bar{\mathcal{J}})^{2}}\bigg)dUdV+\frac{\bar{\mathcal{L}}}{(1+\lambda\bar{\mathcal{J}})^{2}}\,dV^{2}

A_{U}=\mathcal{J}+\frac{\lambda^{2}\mathcal{J}(\kappa\bar{\mathcal{L}}+\bar{\mathcal{J}}^{2})}{2(1+\lambda\bar{\mathcal{J}})}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;\;A_{V}=-\frac{\lambda(\kappa\bar{\mathcal{L}}+\bar{\mathcal{J}}^{2})}{2(1+\lambda\bar{\mathcal{J}})}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;\;B_{U}=-\frac{\lambda\mathcal{J}}{1+\lambda\bar{\mathcal{J}}}\bigg[\bar{\mathcal{J}}+\frac{\lambda(\kappa\bar{\mathcal{L}}+\bar{\mathcal{J}}^{2})}{2}\bigg]

B_{V}=\frac{\bar{\mathcal{J}}}{1+\lambda\bar{\mathcal{J}}}+\frac{\lambda(\kappa\bar{\mathcal{L}}+\bar{\mathcal{J}}^{2})}{2(1+\lambda\bar{\mathcal{J}})}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\kappa\equiv\frac{k}{16\pi^{2}G\ell}

(4.66)

The latter have been rescaled by an overall factor of
\frac{k}{4\pi}
with respect to the previous subsection, to simplify the notation. The relations (
4.42
) map the expectation values in the deformed theory without sources to the holographic
expectation values encoded in this bulk solution as

T^{[\lambda]}_{UU}=\frac{\mathcal{L}}{8\pi G\ell}+\frac{2\pi}{k}\mathcal{J}^{2}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;\;T^{[\lambda]}_{VU}=0\;,\;\;\;\;\;T^{[\lambda]}_{UV}=\lambda\mathcal{J}T_{VV}^{[\lambda]}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;T^{[\lambda]}_{VV}=\frac{1}{1+\lambda\bar{\mathcal{J}}}\bigg(\frac{\bar{\mathcal{L}}}{8\pi G\ell\,}+\frac{2\pi}{k}\bar{\mathcal{J}}^{2}\bigg)

K_{\alpha}\equiv\frac{1}{2}(J^{\prime[\lambda]}+J^{[\lambda]})_{\alpha}=\left(\mathcal{J},\,0\,\right)\;,\;\;\;\;\;\bar{K}_{\alpha}\equiv\frac{1}{2}(J^{\prime[\lambda]}-J^{[\lambda]})_{\alpha}=\left(\frac{\lambda\mathcal{J}\bar{\mathcal{J}}}{1+\lambda\bar{\mathcal{J}}},\,\frac{\bar{\mathcal{J}}}{1+\lambda\bar{\mathcal{J}}}\,\right)

(4.67)

where the computation of the current components is explained in the footnote
9
, and also
[
144
]
. While this calculation was performed in pure
3d
gravity coupled to Chern-Simons gauge fields (a nondynamical theory), the asymptotic expansions of the metric and gauge fields obeying the boundary conditions (
4.54
) is unaffected by the inclusion of matter field expectation values, which change the form of the expansion at subleading orders only.

Checks of the dictionary

As in the
T\bar{T}
case, the simplest check is to compute the entropy-to-energy relation for black hole backgrounds, parametrised by constant
\mathcal{L},\bar{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{J},\bar{\mathcal{J}}
. Integrating the expressions (
4.67
) over
\sigma
, we obtain
10
10

10

The same expression for the energies and conserved charges can also be obtained using the covariant phase space formalism, yielding a non-trivial check of the holographic formulae (
4.67
).

E_{L}=\int d\sigma T_{tU}^{[\lambda]}=\left(\frac{\mathcal{L}}{8\pi G\ell}+\frac{2\pi}{k}\mathcal{J}^{2}\right)R\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;E_{R}=-\int d\sigma T_{tV}^{[\lambda]}=\left(\frac{\bar{\mathcal{L}}}{8\pi G\ell}+\frac{2\pi}{k}\bar{\mathcal{J}}^{2}\right)R_{v}

(4.68)

Q_{L}\equiv\int d\sigma K_{t}=\mathcal{J}R\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;Q_{R}\equiv\int d\sigma\bar{K}_{t}=-\bar{\mathcal{J}}R_{v}

(4.69)

where we introduced the convenient notation
R_{v}
, standing for ‘radius of the field-dependent coordinate’

R_{v}=R-\lambda w\;,\;\;\;\;\;w\equiv\int d\sigma\,\partial_{\sigma}\phi=\frac{R(\mathcal{J}+\bar{\mathcal{J}})}{1+\lambda\bar{\mathcal{J}}}=Q_{L}-Q_{R}

(4.70)

As its definition indicates,
w
is the winding of the field
\phi
, an integer. We note that the parametrisation in terms of
\mathcal{J},\bar{\mathcal{J}}
is not particularly adept at capturing this simple fact.

For a black hole,
\mathcal{L},\bar{\mathcal{L}}
are positive. The horizon is located at
\rho=(\ell^{2}\mathcal{L}\bar{\mathcal{L}})^{-1/2}
and the Bekenstein-Hawking entropy reads

S=\frac{1}{4G}(\sqrt{\mathcal{L}}R+\sqrt{\bar{\mathcal{L}}}R_{v})=\sqrt{\frac{\pi\ell}{2G}}\bigg(\sqrt{RE_{L}-\frac{2\pi}{k}Q_{L}^{2}}+\sqrt{E_{R}R_{v}-\frac{2\pi}{k}Q_{R}^{2}}\bigg)

(4.71)

where in the second step we have rewritten the answer in terms of the conserved charges (
4.68
). Trading
\ell/G
in the prefactor for the Brown-Henneaux central charge
c=\frac{3\ell}{2G}
, we find precisely the universal entropy formula (
3.100
) for a
J\bar{T}
-deformed CFT as a function of the (deformed) conserved charges. Note this formula makes no use of, nor reference to, which charges are quantized, so our identification of the currents in (
4.67
) with the ones whose associated charges are
Q_{L,R}
was important.

While the above match is satisfactory, one would also like to obtain the relation between the deformed and undeformed observables, such as the energies and the charges. For this, one may use the fact that
the entropy, the angular momentum and the winding charge must not vary with
\lambda
: the first, because the deformation is adiabatic and does not change the number of states, while the latter two, because the corresponding charges are quantized. To fully determine the deformed spectrum, which depends on four parameters, one needs one more
\lambda
- independent quantity. The proposal of
[
144
]
is that this is the charge associated to the combination
J^{\prime[0]\alpha}+\frac{k}{2\pi}\epsilon_{[0]}^{\alpha\beta}\mathrm{a}_{\beta}
which, using (
4.44
), is exactly conserved. One thus expects its charge to stay constant along the flow, and equal the quantized shift charge in the undeformed CFT.

Denoting the conserved charges in the deformed theory as
Q_{L},Q_{R}
and in the undeformed one as
J_{0},\bar{J}_{0}
, the relations below follow

Q_{L}-Q_{R}=w=J_{0}-\bar{J}_{0}\;,\;\;\;\;Q_{L}+Q_{R}-\frac{\lambda k}{2\pi}E_{R}=J_{0}+\bar{J}_{0}

(4.72)

which allow us to derive the known expressions for
Q_{L,R}
in the deformed theory in terms of the quantized charges
J_{0},\bar{J}_{0}
. It turns out that, in the bulk, the above conditions are equivalent to requiring that the holonomies of the gauge fields
A,B
around the
\sigma
direction

\displaystyle\frac{k}{4\pi}\oint A_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=Q_{L}-\frac{\lambda k}{4\pi}E_{R}=J_{0}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{k}{4\pi}\oint B_{\sigma}=-Q_{R}+\frac{\lambda k}{4\pi}=-\bar{J}_{0}

(4.73)

do not change with
\lambda
. See
[
144
]
for more details. The formula for the deformed energies in terms of the undeformed ones follows from the four constraints we described.

Since the bulk spectrum perfectly matches that of a
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFT, obviously the thermodynamics should also match. The relationship between the thermodynamic quantities of the deformed theory - which are to be read off from the smoothness conditions for the analytically continued deformed spacetime - and those of the undeformed one - read off from the smoothness of the euclideanised charged BTZ solution - can be obtained by noting the two solutions are simply related by the coordinate transformation (
4.56
), accompanied by the gauge transformation (
4.65
).

In the bulk, the fact that the deformed solution written in terms of the field-dependent coordinates is identical to the undeformed one with some parameters
\mathcal{L},\bar{\mathcal{L}}
implies that the identifications of the field-dependent rescaled coordinates
\hat{u}=\frac{U}{R},\hat{v}=\frac{v}{R_{v}}
are identical to the identifications of the corresponding rescaled coordinates in the undeformed geometry

\displaystyle\hat{u}\sim\hat{u}+m+\frac{in}{RT_{L}^{[0]}}\;,\hskip 28.45274pt\hat{v}\sim\hat{v}+m-\frac{in}{RT_{R}^{[0]}}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;m,n\in\mathbb{Z}

(4.74)

where
T^{[0]}_{L,R}
are related to the parameters of the background in the standard way, fixed by smoothness

\frac{1}{T_{L}^{[0]}}=\frac{\pi\ell}{\sqrt{\mathcal{L}}}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\frac{1}{T_{R}^{[0]}}=\frac{\pi\ell R}{R_{v}\sqrt{\bar{\mathcal{L}}}}

(4.75)

Remember
v
is related to
V
via the shift
v=V-\lambda\phi+const
, where for constant solutions

\displaystyle\phi(U,V)

\displaystyle=\frac{\frac{Q_{L}R_{v}}{R}U-Q_{R}V}{R-\lambda Q_{L}}

(4.76)

which simply follows from requiring that the charges of the conserved currents associated with
\phi
be given by
Q_{L,R}
. Then, using the relation between the two sets of coordinates:
\{\hat{u},\hat{v}\}
and
\{U,V\}
, which can be conveniently written as

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle=(R-\lambda Q_{L})\hat{v}+\lambda Q_{L}\hat{u}\hskip 28.45274ptU=\hat{u}R

(4.77)

one can immediately read off the deformed temperatures, associated to the periodicities of the latter. More precisely, the periodicity of the
U
coordinate yields

\displaystyle T_{L}=T_{L}^{[0]}=\frac{\sqrt{\mathcal{L}}}{\pi\ell}

(4.78)

From the periodicity of
V
we obtain:

\displaystyle V+m(R-\lambda Q_{L}+\lambda Q_{L})-in\bigg(\frac{R-\lambda Q_{L}}{RT_{R}^{[0]}}-\frac{\lambda Q_{L}}{RT_{L}^{[0]}}\bigg)=V+mR-\frac{in}{T_{R}}

(4.79)

The middle terms trivially agree, while the identification of the last terms gives:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T_{R}}

\displaystyle=\frac{R-\lambda Q_{L}}{RT_{R}^{[0]}}-\frac{\lambda Q_{L}}{RT_{L}^{[0]}}

(4.80)

which is precisely the relation (
3.105
) between deformed and undeformed temperatures in the boundary theory. One can also match the
chemical potentials to their field-theoretical values (
3.106
).

4.4
Asymptotic symmetries

In this section, we would like to determine the asymptotic symmetries of AdS
3
gravity with the mixed boundary conditions that correspond to
T\bar{T}
and, respectively,
J\bar{T}
- deformed boundary CFTs. This will give us a first glimpse into the extended symmetries of these very special non-local field theories.

Since the boundary conditions on the bulk fields are known - being fully determined by the dual field theory - determining the asymptotic symmetry group should just be a simple exercise in three steps:

1.

determine the most general diffeomorphisms that obey the boundary conditions

2.

compute the conserved charges, using one’s favourite formalism

3.

compute the charge algebra by varying the charge with respect to another diffeomorphism

In the case of
J\bar{T}
, where gauge fields must be present in the bulk, one must also take into account the corresponding gauge transformations in the first step, in addition to diffeomorphisms.

Despite the apparent simplicity of the above recipe, there are several technical - as well as conceptual - complications. For example, since
the holographic analyses we performed gave us access to the expectation values of the field theory currents (namely, (
4.27
) for
T\bar{T}
and (
4.67
) for
J\bar{T}
), one may think the simplest way to compute the conserved charges is to dot these currents into the components of the allowed diffeomorphisms or gauge transformations, as we did for computing the global conserved charges. However, for a general allowed diffeomorphism the resulting current is not conserved, and so
[
149
]
, where this method was used, had to propose a related, but different vector in order to obtain a conserved charge. The same method was used in
[
178
]
for
J\bar{T}
. While their answer for the charges was ultimately correct, it seems important to be able to derive the expression for the charges from first principles and fully trace the link between bulk diffeomorphisms/gauge transformations and the functions that parametrise the conserved charges.

For this reason, we turn to the covariant phase space formalism, since it provides an entirely algorithmic procedure, with minimal ambiguities, for constructing the conserved charges, once the action and the boundary conditions on the fields are given. As we will see, the results - first worked out in the appendix of
[
255
]
for
T\bar{T}
, and in
[
144
]
for
J\bar{T}
- will turn out to perfectly match an entirely independent field-theoretical analysis that we present in section
5
.

T\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs

Let us now apply the above steps to the holographic dual to
T\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs, namely AdS
3
with the mixed boundary conditions (
4.20
). We work, for simplicity, with pure
3d
gravity, though our analysis - which is performed at infinity - should be equally valid in presence of non-trivial expectation values for the matter fields.

1. The allowed diffeomorphisms

The gravitational phase space is given in (
4.26
) which, under our assumption above, determine the full metric via (
4.29
). Thus, the most general bulk solution is parametrized by two arbitrary (periodic) functions
\mathcal{L}(u),\bar{\mathcal{L}}(v)
of the auxiliary coordinates
u,v
defined in (
4.25
). The most general diffeomorphisms that preserve the radial gauge of the background and the boundary conditions (
4.20
) read
[
149
]

\xi^{U}=f(u)+\hat{\mu}\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{\bar{f}}(v)+\frac{\ell(\rho+\hat{\mu})(\rho\bar{\mathcal{L}}f^{\prime\prime}(u)-\bar{f}^{\prime\prime}(v))}{8\pi G(1-\rho^{2}\mathcal{L}\bar{\mathcal{L}})}

\xi^{V}=\bar{f}(v)+\hat{\mu}\mathcal{L}_{f}(u)+\frac{\ell(\rho+\hat{\mu})(\rho\mathcal{L}\bar{f}^{\prime\prime}(v)-f^{\prime\prime}(u))}{8\pi G(1-\rho^{2}\mathcal{L}\bar{\mathcal{L}})}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;\xi^{\rho}=\rho(f^{\prime}(u)+\bar{f}^{\prime}(v))

(4.81)

where
f,\bar{f}
are arbitrary functions of the field-dependent coordinates and
\mathcal{L}_{f}
,
\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{\bar{f}}
are defined as

\mathcal{L}_{f}(u)=\int^{u}\!\!\!\!du^{\prime}\mathcal{L}(u^{\prime})f^{\prime}(u^{\prime})\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{\bar{f}}(v)=\int^{v}\!\!\!\!dv^{\prime}\bar{\mathcal{L}}(v^{\prime})\bar{f}^{\prime}(v^{\prime})

(4.82)

Intuitively, these are the diffeomorphisms that leave invariant the metric on the
\rho=-\hat{\mu}
surface, and have precisely this bulk interpretation if
\mu<0
.

In principle,
\mathcal{L}_{f},\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{\bar{f}}
are only defined up to an additive constant, as is the relation (
4.25
) between
U,V
and
u,v
. We denote these constants as
c_{\mathcal{L}_{f}}
,
c_{\mathcal{L}}
and their barred counterparts, whereas the primitives are defined to not have a zero mode. These constants, which are in general field-dependent, turn out to play an important role in the integrability of the conserved charges and in the charge algebra.

An important observation made in
[
255
]
is that, since the dual
T\bar{T}
- deformed CFT can be pictured as living on the
\rho=-\hat{\mu}
surface,
the diffeomorphisms (
4.81
) act on the
T\bar{T}
coordinates
U,V
as

U\rightarrow U+f(u)+\hat{\mu}\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{\bar{f}}(v)\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;\;V\rightarrow V+\bar{f}(v)+\hat{\mu}\mathcal{L}_{f}(u)

(4.83)

obtained by setting
\rho=-\hat{\mu}
in (
4.81
). Since the identifications of
U,V
are fixed, this implies that
f,\bar{f}
have non-trivial winding, proportional to that of
\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{\bar{f}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{f}
. The latter is determined by the zero modes of the integrands in (
4.82
), which turn out to be proportional to the conserved charges associated to the diffeomorphisms parametrised by
f^{\prime}
and, respectively,
\bar{f}^{\prime}
.

2. The conserved charges

The next step is to plug in the above expressions for the diffeomorphisms into the covariant phase space formula for the (difference of) conserved charges, which takes the schematic form

\not{\!\delta}Q_{\xi}=\oint\boldsymbol{k}_{\xi}(g,\delta g)

(4.84)

where
k_{\xi}
is a one-form whose expression for Einstein gravity is well-known (see e.g.
[
41
]
). Importantly, this expression only yields the difference in the corresponding charge between two nearby backgrounds, which is not automatically integrable (hence the
\not{\!\delta}
), but integrability needs to be separately checked.

To evaluate this expression, one needs to carefully keep track of the periodicity and winding of the various contributions. As mentioned above, the functions
f,\bar{f}
consist of a periodic and a winding part

f(u)=f_{p}(u)+w_{f}\,u\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;\bar{f}(v)=\bar{f}_{p}(v)+w_{\bar{f}}\,v

(4.85)

with the windings determined as discussed. Second, while the functions
\mathcal{L}(u),\bar{\mathcal{L}}(v)
must be periodic, their variation - which enters (
4.84
) -
can be split into an intrinsic part and one due to the variation of the field-dependent coordinate

\delta[\mathcal{L}(u)]=[\delta\mathcal{L}_{int}](u)+\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(u)\delta u\;,\;\;\;\;\;\delta\mathcal{L}_{int}(u)=\delta\mathcal{L}^{p}_{int}(u)-u\frac{\delta R_{u}}{R_{u}}\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(u)\;,\;\;\;\;\delta R_{u}=2\mu\delta H_{R}

(4.86)

which includes a winding piece. The intrinsic part, which depends in fact on
\hat{u}=u/R_{u}
, can itself be split into a periodic and a winding part; the former corresponds to simply varying the Fourier coefficients in the Fourier expansion of
\mathcal{L}(u)
. Plugging these decompositions into the covariant phase space formula for the charge difference, carefully taking care of winding terms, zero modes, and many integrations by parts, one finally finds that (
4.84
) reduces to

\not{\!\delta}Q_{\xi_{f,\bar{f}}}=\delta Q_{f_{p}}+\delta\bar{Q}_{\bar{f}_{p}}+\pi\left(c_{\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{\bar{f}}}\delta R_{v}+\delta c_{\bar{\mathcal{L}}}w_{\bar{f}}R_{v}-c_{\mathcal{L}_{f}}\delta R_{u}-\delta c_{\mathcal{L}}w_{f}R_{u}\right)

(4.87)

where

\delta Q_{f_{p}}=\delta\left[\frac{1}{2}\oint d\sigma\partial_{\sigma}u\,f_{p}(\hat{u})\mathcal{L}(u)\right]\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\delta\bar{Q}_{\bar{f}_{p}}=\delta\left[-\frac{1}{2}\oint d\sigma\partial_{\sigma}v\,\bar{f}_{p}(\hat{v})\bar{\mathcal{L}}(v)\right]

(4.88)

and we explicitly assumed that
f_{p},\bar{f}_{p}
depended only on
\hat{u}
and, respectively,
\hat{v}
, but not on any additional field-dependent constant.

There are several important observations to make. First, even though the functions
f,\bar{f}
that parametrise the diffeomorphism have winding, the conserved charges only depend on their periodic parts,
f_{p},\bar{f}_{p}
. Their expression coincides with that obtained via an educated guess in
[
149
]
. Having established the expression for the charges, the winding pieces of
f,\bar{f}
can be written in terms of them as

w_{f}=\frac{\mu R_{v}}{\pi RR_{H}}\,\bar{Q}_{\bar{f}_{p}^{\prime}}\,+\frac{\mu^{2}H_{R}}{\pi^{2}RR_{H}}Q_{f_{p}^{\prime}}\;,\;\;\;\;\;w_{\bar{f}}=-\frac{\mu R_{u}}{\pi RR_{H}}\,Q_{f_{p}^{\prime}}\,-\frac{\mu^{2}H_{L}}{\pi^{2}RR_{H}}\bar{Q}_{\bar{f}_{p}^{\prime}}

(4.89)

as advertised, where
R_{H}=R+2\mu H
.

Second, the charges can be made integrable by appropriately choosing the constants
c_{\mathcal{L}}
, etc. Clearly, if
f_{p},\bar{f}_{p}
are just functions of
\hat{u},\hat{v}
, with no additional dependence on field-dependent constants, then an integrable choice is to set all these constants to zero. We will discuss another interesting choice shortly.

3. The charge algebra

In the covariant phase space formalism, the charge algebra (
2.15
) is given by
\delta_{\chi}Q_{\xi}
: the change in the charge associated to an allowed diffeomorphism,
\xi
, between
two backgrounds that differ by another diffeomorphism,
\chi
. In our case, both diffeomorphisms are
of the form (
4.81
). We will denote the two functions that label
\chi
, which acts on the background, as
g(u),\bar{g}(v)
.

The technical subtlety one encounters in this computation is the following: given an initial background parametrised by two functions
\mathcal{L}(u)
,
\bar{\mathcal{L}}(v)
, the change in it induced by acting with a diffeomorphism
\chi
is fully determined by the Lie derivative; thus
\delta_{\chi}\mathcal{L}
and
\delta_{\chi}\bar{\mathcal{L}}
are known, and their explicit expression can already be found in
[
149
]
. However, what enters the charge variation is the periodic part of
\delta\mathcal{L}_{int}
defined in (
4.86
), which requires knowledge of how to split the total known
\delta_{\chi}\mathcal{L}
into an intrinsic contribution, and one due to the change in the field-dependent coordinate. While the functional dependence of the two terms leads to a natural choice, there is still an ambiguity in shifting
\delta u
by a constant,
\delta c_{u}
, which leads to a shift by
-\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\delta c_{u}
in
\delta\mathcal{L}_{int}
. A similar ambiguity exists for the right-movers.

The point is now that these constant ambiguities in
\delta_{\chi}u,\delta_{\chi}v
enter the variation of the integration constants
c_{\mathcal{L}}
, etc. we have introduced above; the precise relations read

\delta_{\chi}c_{\mathcal{L}}=c_{\mathcal{L}_{g}}+\frac{Q_{g_{p}}}{\pi R_{u}}+\frac{\delta_{\chi}c_{v}}{\mu}-\frac{H_{L}}{\pi R_{u}}\delta_{\chi}c_{u}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;\delta_{\chi}c_{\bar{\mathcal{L}}}=c_{\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{\bar{g}}}-\frac{\bar{Q}_{\bar{g}_{p}}}{\pi R_{v}}+\frac{\delta_{\chi}c_{u}}{\mu}-\frac{H_{R}}{\pi R_{v}}\delta_{\chi}c_{v}

(4.90)

We immediately note that it is not possible to set all these constant ambiguities to zero.
For our previous choice of integration constants,
\delta c_{\mathcal{L}}=c_{\mathcal{L}_{f}}=0
(which worked when
f_{p}
was only a function of
\hat{u}
), we can immediately determine

\delta_{\chi}c_{u},\delta_{\chi}c_{v}
in terms of the conserved charges
Q_{g_{p}},\bar{Q}_{\bar{g}_{p}}
. These, in turn, will contribute to the charge algebra via their contribution to
\delta_{\chi}\mathcal{L}_{int}
, and additional contributions will come from the winding terms of
g,\bar{g}
, which involve
Q_{g^{\prime}_{p}},\bar{Q}_{\bar{g}^{\prime}_{p}}
, as per (
4.89
). The end result, worked out in the appendix of
[
255
]
, is the following
non-linear
algebra
11
11

11

To present it, it is useful to split the commutation relation between left-movers and right-movers, by associating
Q_{f}
to the case where
f_{p}\neq 0
and
\bar{f}_{p}=0
, and the reverse for
\bar{Q}_{\bar{f}}
; note that in both cases, the winding parts of
f
and
\bar{f}
are non-zero, being determined by the periodic parts via (
4.89
).

\{Q_{f},Q_{g}\}=Q_{f_{p}g_{p}^{\prime}-g_{p}f_{p}^{\prime}}+\frac{\mu^{2}H_{R}}{\pi^{2}RR_{H}}(Q_{f}Q_{g^{\prime}}-Q_{g}Q_{f^{\prime}})-\frac{\ell}{16\pi G}\oint du\,f_{p}(u)g_{p}^{\prime\prime\prime}(u)

\{\bar{Q}_{\bar{f}},\bar{Q}_{\bar{g}}\}=\bar{Q}_{\bar{f}_{p}\bar{g}_{p}^{\prime}-\bar{g}_{p}\bar{f}_{p}^{\prime}}-\frac{\mu^{2}H_{L}}{\pi^{2}RR_{H}}(\bar{Q}_{\bar{f}}\bar{Q}_{\bar{g}^{\prime}}-\bar{Q}_{\bar{g}}\bar{Q}_{\bar{f}^{\prime}})-\frac{\ell}{16\pi G}\oint dv\,\bar{f}_{p}(v)\bar{g}_{p}^{\prime\prime\prime}(v)

(4.91)

\{Q_{f},\bar{Q}_{\bar{g}}\}=\frac{2\mu}{2\pi RR_{H}}(R_{u}Q_{f^{\prime}}\bar{Q}_{\bar{g}}+R_{v}Q_{f}\bar{Q}_{\bar{g}^{\prime}})

(4.92)

It is useful to rewrite these charges and their commutators in terms of a Fourier basis, where
f_{n}=e^{inu/R_{u}}
and
\bar{f}_{n}=-e^{-inv/R_{v}}
. We find

i\{Q_{m},Q_{n}\}=\frac{1}{R_{u}}(m-n)Q_{m+n}+\frac{\mu^{2}H_{R}}{\pi^{2}RR_{H}R_{u}}(m-n)Q_{m}Q_{n}+\frac{c\,m^{3}}{12R_{u}^{2}}\delta_{m+n}

i\{\bar{Q}_{m},\bar{Q}_{n}\}=\frac{1}{R_{v}}(m-n)\bar{Q}_{m+n}+\frac{\mu^{2}H_{L}}{\pi^{2}RR_{H}R_{v}}(m-n)\bar{Q}_{m}\bar{Q}_{n}+\frac{c\,m^{3}}{12R_{v}^{2}}\delta_{m+n}

i\{Q_{m},\bar{Q}_{n}\}=-\frac{\mu(m-n)}{\pi RR_{H}}Q_{m}\bar{Q}_{n}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;c=\frac{3\ell}{2G}

(4.93)

The semi-classical commutation relations are obtained by the replacement
i\{\;,\}\rightarrow\hbar^{-1}[\;,]
. The result is a Poisson algebra that is a non-linear modification of two copies of the centrally-extended Virasoro algebra, to which it reduces when
\mu=0
. To see the non-linearities, it is important to be working in finite size. Note that, since we obtained this result from a semiclassical calculation, we expect the above to yield only the first term in the expansion of the full quantum commutator with respect to
\hbar
, as in principle a Poisson algebra can contain arbitrary powers of the generators; in particular, in the full quantum theory the ordering of the generators that appear in the non-linear correction would need to be specified.

Another interesting choice of integration constants is
\delta c_{u,v}=\delta c_{\mathcal{L},\bar{\mathcal{L}}}=0
, case in which (
4.90
) implies

c_{\mathcal{L}_{f}}=-\frac{Q_{f}}{\pi R_{u}}\;,\;\;\;\;\;c_{\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{\bar{f}}}=\frac{\bar{Q}_{\bar{f}}}{\pi R_{v}}

(4.94)

The expression (
4.87
) now appears to no longer be integrable; however, it is easy to see that, by simply multiplying
f_{p}
by
R_{u}
and
\bar{f}_{p}
by
R_{v}
, the charge associated to these rescaled diffeomorphisms will be integrable. The algebra of these charges associated to the rescaled diffeomorphisms will be simpler to compute, since the
\delta c_{u,v}
contribution is absent; as it turns out, the winding contributions also cancel, and the end result is simply two commuting copies of the Virasoro algebra

\delta_{R_{u}g}Q_{R_{u}f}=R_{u}Q_{R_{u}(f_{p}g_{p}^{\prime}-g_{p}f_{p}^{\prime})}-\frac{R_{u}^{2}\ell}{16\pi G}\oint duf_{p}g_{p}^{\prime\prime\prime}

(4.95)

with the same central extension as the undeformed CFT. It is to be expected that this algebra will not receive further quantum corrections. In the semiclassical limit, the Virasoro generators are simply rescaled versions, by a factor of
R_{u}
, of the first ones we discussed, (
4.88
); however, their relation could become more complicated in the full quantum theory.

Note that from a field-theoretical perspective, it is obvious
that multiplying the generators by a factor of the field-dependent radius, which depends on the Hamiltonian, will change their algebra. However, in the covariant phase space formalism, this multiplicative factor is simply seen as a
c
-number, and it is the integrability of the conserved charges that ensures that the correct algebra of the rescaled generators is reproduced, via the subtle contributions of integration constants that it constrains.

Thus, the ASG analysis of AdS
3
gravity with the mixed boundary conditions that correspond to the
T\bar{T}
deformation of the dual CFT shows that in a certain basis, the asymptotic symmetries organise themselves into two commuting copies of the Virasoro algebra, with the same central extension as in the undeformed CFT. Since in the holographic context, the ASG of the bulk gravitational theory is identified with the symmetries of its boundary QFT dual, this calculation strongly suggests that at least in the classical large
c
limit,
T\bar{T}
-deformed CFTs possess full Virasoro
\times
Virasoro symmetry. These symmetries are however unusual, in that they depend on the field configuration, and also that in the natural Fourier basis for the generators, the symmetry algebra corresponds to a non-linear modification of Virasoro
2
. If the existence of these symmetries is confirmed at non-perturbative level, it could lead to an interpretation of
T\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs as non-local generalizations of usual CFTs.

J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs

The analysis of asymptotic symmetries in the spacetimes dual to
J\bar{T}
-deformed CFTs is entirely analogous to the one above, though slightly more complicated due to the larger number of fields involved.

We are interested in the asymptotic symmetries of Einstein gravity in AdS
3
coupled to the two Chern-Simons gauge fields (
4.45
), subject to the mixed boundary conditions (
4.54
). As discussed, the most general background satisfying these boundary conditions is conveniently parametrized by four arbitrary
periodic
functions
\mathcal{L}(U),\bar{\mathcal{L}}(v),\mathcal{J}(U),\bar{\mathcal{J}}(v)
, where
v
is a field-dependent coordinate that satisfies

\partial_{U}v=-\lambda\partial_{U}\phi=-\frac{\lambda\mathcal{J}(U)}{1+\lambda\bar{\mathcal{J}}(v)}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\partial_{V}v=1-\lambda\partial_{V}\phi=\frac{1}{1+\lambda\bar{\mathcal{J}}(v)}

(4.96)

as follows from (
4.62
) and (
4.63
).
The scalar field
\phi
is the bosonisation (
4.43
) of the
U(1)
current entering the
J\bar{T}
deformation.
One may formally integrate the above equations and
express
v
in terms of the primitives of
\mathcal{J}(U),\bar{\mathcal{J}}(v)
as

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=V-\lambda\left(\int^{U}dU^{\prime}\mathcal{J}(U^{\prime})+\int^{v}dv^{\prime}\bar{\mathcal{J}}(v^{\prime})+c_{v}\right)

(4.97)

where the integrals are defined to not contain any constant mode, placing instead this ambiguity in the integration constant
c_{v}
which,
a priori
, can be field-dependent.

1. Allowed diffeomorphisms and gauge transformations

We work in radial gauge for both the metric and the gauge fields. The most general such diffeomorphism is parametrised by three functions of
U,V
only

\xi=zF_{z}(U,V)\partial_{z}+\left(F_{U}(U,V)+\ldots\right)\partial_{U}+(F_{V}(U,V)+\ldots)\partial_{V}

(4.98)

where the
\ldots
stand for terms proportional to derivatives of
F_{z}
and we changed the radial coordinate to
z=\sqrt{\rho}
. To keep the gauge fields in radial gauge, one needs to perform a compensating gauge transformation that depends on the above functions (and the background); in addition, one can perform
z
- independent gauge transformations, which will be parametrised by two additional functions,
G_{A,B}(U,V)
.

The requirement that the background generated by this diffeomorphism and gauge transformations satisfy the mixed boundary conditions associated to
J\bar{T}
fixes the form of these functions to

F_{U}(U,V)=f-\frac{\ell^{2}f^{\prime\prime}}{2\mathcal{L}}\;,\;\;\;\;\;F_{V}(U,V)=\bar{f}-\lambda\eta-\lambda\bar{\eta}-\frac{\lambda\ell^{2}\mathcal{J}f^{\prime\prime}}{2\mathcal{L}}-\frac{(1+\lambda\bar{\mathcal{J}})\ell^{2}\bar{f}^{\prime\prime}}{2\bar{\mathcal{L}}}\;,\;\;\;\;\;F_{z}(U,V)=\frac{f^{\prime}+\bar{f}^{\prime}}{2}

G_{A}(U,V)=\frac{\mathcal{J}\ell^{2}f^{\prime\prime}}{2\mathcal{L}}-\frac{\lambda\bar{\mathcal{J}}^{2}\ell^{2}\bar{f}^{\prime\prime}}{4\bar{\mathcal{L}}}+\eta-\lambda\int^{v}\bar{\mathcal{J}}\bar{\eta}^{\prime}-\frac{\lambda}{2}\int^{v}(\kappa\bar{\mathcal{L}}+\bar{\mathcal{J}}^{2})\bar{f}^{\prime}

G_{B}(U,V)=\frac{\bar{\mathcal{J}}(2+\lambda\bar{\mathcal{J}})\ell^{2}\bar{f}^{\prime\prime}}{4\bar{\mathcal{L}}}+\bar{\eta}+\lambda\int^{v}\bar{\mathcal{J}}\bar{\eta}^{\prime}+\frac{\lambda}{2}\int^{v}(\kappa\bar{\mathcal{L}}+\bar{\mathcal{J}}^{2})\bar{f}^{\prime}

(4.99)

where
f(U),\eta(U),\bar{f}(v),\bar{\eta}(v)
are arbitrary functions of the indicated coordinates.

As before, it is important to determine the periodicity conditions on these functions. The main condition stems from the requirement that the holonomies of the gauge fields
A,B
around the
\sigma
circle be fixed; as we saw in the previous subsection, these holonomies are related to the undeformed charges
J_{0},\bar{J}_{0}
, which are quantized and should thus not be affected by continuous symmetry transformations. This in turn implies that the functions
G_{A,B}
have no winding. Taking the sum and difference of the equations (
4.99
), we find that
\eta
and
\bar{\eta}
have equal, but opposite winding

\eta=\eta_{p}(\hat{U})+w_{\eta}\,\hat{U}\hskip 28.45274pt\bar{\eta}=\bar{\eta}_{p}(\hat{v})-w_{\eta}\,\hat{v}

(4.100)

with

w_{\eta}=\frac{1}{[1+\lambda\bar{\mathcal{J}}]_{zm}}\left(\frac{\lambda}{2}[(\kappa\bar{\mathcal{L}}+\bar{\mathcal{J}}^{2})\bar{f}^{\prime}]_{zm}+\lambda[\bar{\mathcal{J}}\bar{\eta}^{\prime}_{p}]_{zm}\right)=-\frac{1}{R_{Q}}\left(\frac{\lambda\hat{k}}{2}\bar{Q}_{\bar{f}^{\prime}}+\lambda\bar{P}_{\bar{\eta}^{\prime}}\right)

(4.101)

where
\eta_{p},\bar{\eta}_{p}
are periodic,
\hat{U}=U/R,\hat{v}=v/R_{v}
and
[~~]_{zm}
denotes the zero mode of the corresponding quantity, which we will soon show is proportional to the conserved charges associated to these symmetries. Thus, the requirement that the charges
J_{0},\bar{J}_{0}
be fixed translates into a charge-dependent winding for these functions. One can easily see that
f,\bar{f}
are both periodic.

2. The conserved charges

The conserved charges are obtained by simply plugging in the above general allowed diffeomorphism and gauge transformation in the appropriate covariant phase space formula for the conserved charges, which now receives contributions from both the Einstein and the Chern-Simons terms. See
[
144
]
for the precise formulae. It is useful to split the conserved charges in four categories, labeled by the four functions we have introduced:

•

Left affine charges:

\xi_{\eta}=-\frac{\lambda\hat{k}}{2}\eta_{p}(U)\partial_{V}\,,\;\Lambda_{A}=\frac{\hat{k}}{2}\eta_{p}(U)\,,\;\Lambda_{B}=0\,;\;P_{\eta}=\int d\sigma\;\eta_{p}(U)\mathcal{J}(U)

(4.102)

•

Left conformal charges:
only
f(U)
turned on in (
4.99
);
Q_{f}=\int d\sigma f(U)\left(\frac{\mathcal{L}}{8\pi G\ell}+\frac{2\pi}{k}\mathcal{J}^{2}\right)

•

Right affine charges:

\eta=w_{\eta}\hat{U},\;\bar{\eta}=\bar{\eta}_{p}-w_{\eta}\hat{v},\;f=\bar{f}=0
;
\bar{P}_{\bar{\eta}}=-\int d\sigma\bar{\eta}_{p}\bar{\mathcal{J}}\partial_{\sigma}v

•

Right pseudoconformal charges:

\eta=w_{\eta}\hat{U},\;\bar{\eta}=-w_{\eta}\hat{v},\;f=0
;
\bar{Q}_{\bar{f}}=-\int d\sigma\partial_{\sigma}v\bar{f}\left(\frac{\bar{\mathcal{L}}}{8\pi G\ell}+\frac{2\pi}{k}\bar{\mathcal{J}}^{2}\right)

where
w_{\eta}
is determined by the right-moving function that is turned on via the general formula (
4.101
).
As before, only the periodic part of the functions enters the conserved charges. The way the latter were computed is of course via the charge differences
/\penalty{\!\!\!\delta}Q
, but these can be shown to be integrable on the phase space. Quite naturally, each type of function leads to a conserved charge, except that for the right-moving asymptotic symmetries we are forced to turn on equal and opposite windings for both
\eta,\bar{\eta}
due to the charge constraint.

Note also that the left-moving affine transformations, where the transformation was short enough for us to write it down explicitly, consists of both the expected gauge transformation and a right-moving translation. This is quite natural from the Hamiltonian perspective, since the left affine currents (
3.145
) contain a factor of the right-moving Hamiltonian; it will also be important shortly, when we compare this analysis with that of the CSS boundary conditions.

3. The asymptotic symmetry group

The charge algebra is computed via the same steps as for the holographic dual to
T\bar{T}
. One again encounters the subtlety of how to split the variations of
\delta\bar{\mathcal{J}}
and
\delta\bar{\mathcal{L}}
, if applicable, into an ‘intrinsic’ part and one due to the variation of the field-dependent coordinate, resulting in an ambiguity parametrised by a field-dependent constant. This ambiguity can be fixed by requiring that the bracket be antisymmetric. Taking into account also the winding contributions and decomposing the functions into the appropriate Fourier modes,
e^{2\pi imU/R}
and
e^{-2\pi inv/R_{v}}
, one finds

\{P_{m},P_{n}\}=-\frac{im\hat{k}}{2}\delta_{n+m,0}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\{Q_{m},Q_{n}\}=-\frac{2\pi i(m-n)}{R}Q_{m+n}\;,\;\;\;\;\{Q_{m},P_{n}\}=\frac{2\pi in}{R}P_{m+n}

\{\bar{P}_{m},\bar{P}_{n}\}=-\frac{im\hat{k}}{2}\delta_{m+n,0}-\frac{im\hat{k}\lambda}{2R_{Q}}\bar{P}_{m}\delta_{n,0}+\frac{in\hat{k}\lambda}{2R_{Q}}\bar{P}_{n}\delta_{m,0}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\{\bar{Q}_{m},\bar{P}_{n}\}=\frac{2\pi in}{R_{v}}\bar{P}_{m+n}+\frac{2\pi in\lambda}{R_{Q}R_{v}}\bar{P}_{n}\bar{P}_{m}-\frac{im\hat{k}\lambda}{R_{Q}}\bar{Q}_{m}\delta_{n,0}

\{\bar{Q}_{m},\bar{Q}_{n}\}=-\frac{2\pi i(m-n)\bar{Q}_{m+n}}{R_{v}}-\frac{2\pi im\lambda}{R_{Q}R_{v}}\bar{Q}_{m}\bar{P}_{n}+\frac{2\pi in\lambda}{R_{Q}R_{v}}\bar{Q}_{n}\bar{P}_{m}+\frac{c}{12}\frac{in^{3}}{(R_{v}/2\pi)^{2}}\delta_{m+n,0}

where
R_{Q}\equiv R-\lambda Q_{L}
and
R_{v}=R-\lambda w
.
The mixed left-right commutators are

\{Q_{m},\bar{Q}_{n}\}=\frac{2\pi in\lambda}{RR_{Q}}\bar{Q}_{n}P_{m}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\{P_{m},\bar{P}_{n}\}=\frac{in\hat{k}\lambda}{2R_{Q}}\bar{P}_{n}\delta_{m,0}

\{P_{m},\bar{Q}_{n}\}=\frac{2\pi in\lambda}{R_{Q}}\bar{Q}_{n}\delta_{m,0}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\{Q_{m},\bar{P}_{n}\}=\frac{2\pi in\lambda}{R_{Q}R}\bar{P}_{n}P_{m}

(4.103)

The algebra of the left-moving generators is simply a Virasoro-Kac-Moody algebra, with the same central extensions as in the undeformed CFT. The algebra of the right-movers is a non-linear modification of Virasoro-Kac-Moody (to which it reduces when
\lambda=0
). This algebra has the special property that, if ones defines the new generators (
H_{R}=\bar{Q}_{0}
)

\tilde{Q}_{m}=Q_{m}-\frac{\lambda H_{R}}{R}P_{m}+\frac{\lambda^{2}\hat{k}H_{R}^{2}}{4R}\delta_{m,0}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\tilde{P}_{m}=P_{m}-\frac{\lambda\hat{k}H_{R}}{2}\delta_{m,0}

\tilde{\bar{Q}}_{m}=\frac{R_{v}}{R}\bar{Q}_{m}-\frac{\lambda H_{R}}{R}\bar{P}_{m}+\frac{\lambda^{2}\hat{k}H_{R}^{2}}{4R}\delta_{m,0}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\tilde{\bar{P}}_{m}=\bar{P}_{m}-\frac{\lambda\hat{k}H_{R}}{2}\delta_{m,0}

(4.104)

their algebra consists precisely of two commuting copies of the Virasoro-Kac-Moody algebra, with the same central extensions as in the undeformed spacetime. Hence, as in the
T\bar{T}
case, the asymptotic symmetry algebra of the spacetimes dual to holographic
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs is a non-linear algebra, but one that can be brought to Virasoro form via a simple non-linear redefinition. In the present case, this non-linear map formally resembles a spectral flow by
\lambda H_{R}
. As we will show, this result matches perfectly that of the field theory analysis in the next section.

Comments on AdS
3
gravity with CSS boundary conditions

Throughout this section, we have been emphasizing the fact that, unlike most holographic studies of AdS
3
with non-standard boundary conditions that exist in the literature, the two holographic dictonaries we have presented are top-down, in the sense of being
derived
from boundary QFTs that have their own,
independent definition
. In particular, the boundary conditions that the bulk fields obey are dictated by the dual field theory and automatically lead to conserved charges that are finite, integrable and conserved, all while allowing for full dynamics for the matter fields that may be present in the bulk. This should be contrasted with the bottom-up approaches, where the boundary conditions on the bulk fields are the result of an educated guess, one may or not be able to include dynamical matter, and the
existence of a boundary theory that posseses the properties one infers from the bulk is always an
assumption
, whose validity depends on the consistency of the setup and of the boundary conditions.

To illustrate this point, we would like to end this section with a comparison between the
J\bar{T}
holographic dictionary we have derived and a
bottom-up
holographic proposal for AdS
3
with very similar non-standard boundary conditions, known as Compère-Song-Strominger (CSS) ones
[
100
]
. More precisely, the backgrounds allowed by the CSS boundary conditions correspond to a truncation of the allowed backgrounds (
4.66
) to the subsector where
\bar{\mathcal{J}}=0
and
\bar{\mathcal{L}}
is required to be constant. The map between the parametrisation of
[
100
]
and ours is

\displaystyle\Delta\leftrightarrow\bar{\mathcal{L}}\;,\hskip 42.67912pt\bar{L}(t^{+})\leftrightarrow\mathcal{L}(U)\;,\hskip 42.67912pt\partial_{+}\bar{P}(t^{+})\leftrightarrow-\lambda\mathcal{J}(U)

(4.105)

Note, however, that the Chern-Simons gauge fields (
4.66
) do not vanish when
\bar{\mathcal{J}}=0
, whereas in the analysis of
[
100
]
, they are simply absent.

One of the main reasons to study
[
100
]
’s setup is their proposal that Einstein gravity with CSS boundary conditions is holographically dual to a warped CFT
[
93
,
28
]
. Remember from section
2.3
that warped CFTs are
putative
two-dimensional QFT with
SL(2,\mathbb{R})_{L}\times U(1)_{R}
global symmetry that is enhanced to a left-moving Virasoro-Kac-Moody algebra, with the peculiarity that the Kac-Moody factor enhances right translations
6
. Or,
[
100
]
found precisely this symmetry enhancement pattern in the asymptotic symmetries of pure
3d
gravity with CSS boundary conditions. However, several peculiarities were present, such as the fact that the
U(1)
level was found to be background dependent (proportional to
\Delta
), and generally negative. Second, the requirement that
\bar{\mathcal{L}}=\Delta
be fixed (or, at most a varying constant) is a very severe restriction on the dynamics of the theory: in a CFT, this would correspond to requiring that all the non-zero Fourier modes of the right-moving stress tensor vanish
12
12

12

In particular,
states of the form
\mathcal{O}(U,V)|0\rangle
, which would yield a non-trivial
V
-dependent profile for the expectation value of the right-moving stress tensor, would not be allowed for any operator
\mathcal{O}
that has a non-trivial right-moving scaling.
. How to include dynamical matter in the CSS phase space is thus not entirely clear.

Given that the backgrounds considered in
[
100
]
correspond to a truncation of the backgrounds (
4.66
), let us try to understand the CSS ASG calculation from the perspective of
J\bar{T}
holography, which is on a significantly firmer footing.
The allowed transformations on the restricted phase space with
\bar{\mathcal{J}}=0
and
\bar{\mathcal{L}}=const.
consist of left affine and left conformal transformations, as well as a constant right-moving translation. It is rather clear that the Virasoro-Kac-Moody symmetries
[
100
]
find correspond to the
J\bar{T}
left-moving Virasoro-Kac-Moody symmetries, though with several differences

•

the
interpretation
of the Kac-Moody symmetries is different, as already remarked in
[
178
]
: while in
J\bar{T}
, the Kac-Moody is an affine
U(1)
symmetry associated to the left
U(1)
current, whose bulk implementation consists of the standard gauge transformations
and
an accompanying right translation dictated by the mixed boundary conditions, in
[
100
]
, since the Chern-Simons gauge fields are absent, only the shift of the right-moving coordinate is visible, which is then interpreted as the enhancement of right translations to left Kac-Moody that is characteristic of warped CFTs

•

the calculations leading to the Virasoro - Kac-Moody symmetries are different; in particular, the
J\bar{T}
calculation leads to a KM level that is constant and positive (the same as in the undeformed CFT), whereas the level found by CSS is state-dependent and usually negative.

To understand how the calculation of
[
100
]
works from the
J\bar{T}
perspective, one can separate the calculation of the Kac-Moody conserved charges (
4.102
) into the contribution coming from the metric sector, which is the same as the one in
[
100
]

[/\penalty{\!\!\!\delta}P_{\eta}]_{metric}=\int d\sigma\frac{\eta(U)}{2}\bigg(-2\lambda^{2}\bar{\mathcal{L}}\delta\mathcal{J}+\lambda(1-\lambda\mathcal{J})\delta\bar{\mathcal{L}}\bigg)

(4.106)

and the contribution of the Chern-Simons terms in the action

[/\penalty{\!\!\!\delta}P_{\eta}]_{CS}=\int_{0}^{R}d\sigma\frac{\eta(U)}{2}\bigg(-\lambda(1-\lambda\mathcal{J})\delta\bar{\mathcal{L}}+2(1+\lambda^{2}\bar{\mathcal{L}})\delta\mathcal{J}\bigg)

(4.107)

If one only considers the metric contribution, then the charge variations are not integrable and one is forced to fix
\bar{\mathcal{L}}
(or
\mathcal{J}
, but we do not consider this possibility), as
[
100
]
did. This seemed overly restrictive, though, and the authors contrived a way to make the charges integrable for
\Delta
a variable constant. The resulting asymptotic symmetry algebra is a
U(1)
Kac-Moody algebra with a
\Delta
(
=\bar{\mathcal{L}}
) - dependent level, which is negative for positive
\bar{\mathcal{L}}
, as can be seen from (
4.106
).

If, on the other hand, one adds in the contribution from the gauge fields, the terms proportional to
\delta\bar{\mathcal{L}}
and
\bar{\mathcal{L}}\delta\mathcal{J}
automatically cancel, so that there is no issue regarding integrability and one can freely vary
\bar{\mathcal{L}}
in the phase space.
The final variation is given by the simple expression (
4.102
), which leads to a Kac-Moody algebra with the same
U(1)
level as in the undeformed CFT. A similar story holds for the left-moving conformal symmetries, see
[
144
]
for details.

Thus, we see that the inclusion of the Chern-Simons gauge fields in the low-energy effective bulk action, with appropriate boundary conditions dictated by the dual
J\bar{T}
deformation, solves both the non-dynamics and the negative
U(1)
level problems associated to the CSS boundary conditions.
Their
inclusion: i) permits relaxing the boundary conditions, so that arbitrary matter field excitations (with reasonable near-boundary behaviour) are allowed, thus solving the dynamics problem, and ii) gives additional contributions to the charges, so that the negative background-dependent
U(1)
level never shows up. This points towards the conclusion that the framework of pure
3d
Einstein gravity is simply not sufficient
to properly model a consistent phase space
for the non-standard boundary conditions put forth in
[
100
]
.
The holographic setup associated with the
J\bar{T}
deformation does provide such a consistent framework, which preserves the full dynamics of the theory; however, this new framework requires the inclusion of a Chern-Simons gauge field dual to the
U(1)
current used to define the
J\bar{T}
deformation. If the original CFT does not contain a
U(1)
current, then we see no obvious way to construct a well-defined double-trace operator that would drive the deformation of the boundary conditions from Dirichlet to CSS by just using the stress tensor
13
13

13

Taking the square root of the left stress tensor to produce a dimension one operator is not a legit quantum operation.
. Another consequence of having to use the
J\bar{T}
framework in order to implement boundary conditions of the type considered in
[
100
]
is that the correct interpretation of the left Kac-Moody asympotic symmetries is as the affine
U(1)
algebra that enhances the left
U(1)
current, rather than the Kac-Moody enhancement of right translations that characterises a warped CFT.

The new dual interpretation of CSS-like boundary conditions is within a class of field theories that are significantly better defined and understood than warped CFTs, for which the only known concrete examples are free
[
94
,
95
]
. As we just saw, the problem with holographic definitions of interacting (strongly-coupled) warped CFTs is that one first needs to ascertain the consistency of the holographic setup, which in general is hard to do. The ultimate test whether a given set of boundary conditions in a low-energy effective field theory - assumed, as usual, to admit a consistent UV completion - are correct or not is whether they match the predictions from an independently-defined and consistent dual field theory. The existence of this dual would likely impose many more constraints on the gravitational phase space than are currently known.

5
Infinite pseudoconformal symmetries of
T\bar{T}
and
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs

As reviewed in section
2.1
, in holography, the asymptotic symmetries of a given spacetime correspond to the global symmetries of the dual field theory. The ASG analyses of the previous section thus make a rather remarkable prediction: that both
T\bar{T}
and
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs should posses an infinite number of symmetries, which obey a certain non-linear modification of the Virasoro
\times
Virasoro or (Virasoro-Kac-Moody)
2
algebra.
Moreover, a simple non-linear change of the basis of symmetry generators turns the algebra into two commuting copies of the Virasoro or Virasoro-Kac-Moody algebra, the same as the symmetry algebra of the undeformed CFT.

The goal of this section is to uncover these symmetries and their peculiar features also from the purely field-theoretical point of view. We start with a Hamiltonian analysis, which is conceptually the simplest, and then translate the results to the Lagrangian formalism, which is closer to holography.

Contents of this section:

5.1
General argument for Virasoro symmetry

The simplest argument that shows the existence of extended symmetries in
T\bar{T}
and
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs is also very general and valid at the full quantum level: consider a family of non-local two-dimensional QFTs, assumed to be UV complete, which are obtained via an integrable irrelevant deformation of a CFT
2
(of central charge
c
), labeled by a continuous parameter
\lambda
. One then places these QFTs on a cylinder of circumference
R
, and assumes they can be simply treated as quantum-mechanical systems. The deformation is assumed to not change the Hilbert space, but only the Hamiltonian and its associated eigenstates, in an adiabatic fashion. The latter should satisfy a smooth flow equation

\partial_{\lambda}|n_{\lambda}\rangle=\hat{\mathcal{X}}|n_{\lambda}\rangle

(5.1)

where
\hat{\mathcal{X}}
is entirely determined by the (known) deformation of the Hamiltonian,
\partial_{\lambda}H
, via

\partial_{\lambda}H=D-[H,\hat{\mathcal{X}}]

(5.2)

where
D
represents the diagonal matrix elements of
\partial_{\lambda}H
in the instantaneous energy eigenbasis. One may then use
\hat{\mathcal{X}}
to formally define a set of generators
\widetilde{L}_{m}^{\lambda}
as
[
133
,
181
,
180
]

\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}\widetilde{L}_{m}^{\lambda}\equiv\partial_{\lambda}\widetilde{L}_{m}^{\lambda}-[\hat{\mathcal{X}},\widetilde{L}_{m}^{\lambda}]=0\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\widetilde{L}_{m}^{\lambda=0}=L_{m}^{CFT}

(5.3)

Of course, there will be one such generator for every generator of extended symmetries in the original CFT; the properties of
\widetilde{L}_{m}^{\lambda}
below will thus hold, with the appropriate modifications, also for the right-moving Virasoro generators, as well as for possible Kac-Moody symmetries that may be present. By construction, the
\widetilde{L}_{m}^{\lambda}
:

i)

are well-defined, since
\hat{\mathcal{X}}
is well-defined

ii)

obey a Virasoro
algebra
, with the same central extension,
c
, as the seed CFT

iii)

are conserved, and therefore correspond to
symmetries
of the theory

To check conservation, one should first define
\widetilde{L}_{n}^{\lambda}(t)
by using the flow operator for the eigenstates on a
t\neq 0
time slice. Since the time dependence of energy eigenstates is simply
e^{-iE_{n}t}
, it immediately follows that
\hat{\mathcal{X}}(t)=\hat{\mathcal{X}}(0)-iDt
. Even though
\widetilde{L}_{n}^{\lambda}(t)
is a Schrödinger picture operator, it has explicit time dependence because
\mathcal{X}(t)
used in its definition does. It can be easily checked then that the
\lambda
derivative of the conservation equation is

\frac{d}{d\lambda}\left(\partial_{t}\widetilde{L}_{m}^{\lambda}+\frac{i}{\hbar}[H,\widetilde{L}_{m}^{\lambda}]\right)=\bigg[\partial_{t}\hat{\mathcal{X}}(t)+\frac{i}{\hbar}\partial_{\lambda}H-\frac{i}{\hbar}[\hat{\mathcal{X}},H],\widetilde{L}_{m}^{\lambda}\bigg]+\biggl[\hat{\mathcal{X}}(t),\partial_{t}\widetilde{L}_{m}+\frac{i}{\hbar}[H,\widetilde{L}_{m}^{\lambda}]\biggr]

(5.4)

Since the
first term on the right-hand-side vanishes via the definition (
5.2
), the above implies that the charge will stay conserved, if it was initially so.

This remarkably simple argument shows that all extended symmetries of the undeformed CFT can be transported along the irrelevant flow to the deformed theory, leaving the symmetry algebra unchanged. One may nonetheless
feel uncomfortable with the generality of the argument, which only uses adiabaticity, and none of the special properties of the
T\bar{T}
or
J\bar{T}
deformation. In particular, this argument seems to apply to any irrelevant or relevant deformation of a CFT, as long as it is adiabatic,
predicting in each case the preservation of the Virasoro symmetries, which is puzzling.

While we currently do not have a precise understanding of when and why this type of argument should hold or fail, let us make a few potentially relevant remarks. First, the construction of the conserved charges via the flow we have described does not guarantee that the result is the integral of a local current. As we will show in the next subsection,
T\bar{T}
and
J\bar{T}
are special in that one can solve for the (classical) flowed currents explicitly and keep track of these non-localities, at least at the classical level. As we will show, the result is more non-local than the expected non-localities of
T\bar{T}
/
J\bar{T}
deformed CFTs. It is thus possible that in more general theories, the symmetries so defined mix IR and UV modes in a way that becomes intractable from the IR point of view.
Second, we have been using a purely quantum-mechanical setup to make the argument; however, it is not clear whether the
UV divergences that are characteristic of QFT may affect the argument
[
211
]
.
For the special case of the
T\bar{T}
and
J\bar{T}
deformations, the consequences derived from the argument above will yield charges that are in perfect agreement with the holographic computations of the previous section. Nonetheless, it would be valuable to have a direct QFT appraisal of its validity.

In the remainder of this section, we will explicitly solve for the flowed currents in
classical

T\bar{T}
and
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs and derive the classical symmetries they generate. But first, let us mention a few more quantum results in
T\bar{T}
and
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs that one can derive from the flow above:

•

the fact that
\widetilde{L}^{\lambda}_{0}
and
\widetilde{\bar{L}}^{\lambda}_{0}
are entirely determined by the Hamiltonian, momentum and charge operators of the deformed theory. More precisely, in
T\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs, one finds
[
133
]

\widetilde{L}_{0}^{\mu}=H_{L}(1+2\mu H_{R})\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;\widetilde{\bar{L}}_{0}^{\mu}=H_{R}(1+2\mu H_{R})

(5.5)

whereas in
J\bar{T}
, where the flowed generators are
\widetilde{L}_{m}^{\lambda},\widetilde{\bar{L}}_{m}^{\lambda},\widetilde{K}_{m}^{\lambda},\widetilde{\bar{K}}_{m}^{\lambda}
, the zero modes read

\widetilde{L}_{0}^{\lambda}=H_{L}-\lambda J_{0}H_{R}-\frac{k\lambda^{2}}{4}H_{R}^{2}\;,\;\;\;\;\widetilde{K}_{0}=J_{0}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\widetilde{\bar{L}}_{0}^{\lambda}=H_{R}-\lambda J_{0}H_{R}-\frac{k\lambda^{2}}{4}H_{R}^{2}\;,\;\;\;\;\widetilde{\bar{K}}_{0}=\bar{J}_{0}

(5.6)

•

the exact time dependence of the Schrödinger picture generators
\widetilde{L}^{\lambda}_{n}
entering (
5.4
)

The expressions (
5.5
)-(
5.6
) can be derived by either noting that the states
\widetilde{L}^{\lambda}_{0}|n_{\lambda}\rangle
and
|n_{\lambda}\rangle
flow in the same way with
\lambda
, implying that the constant of proportionality between them is the same as in the undeformed CFT, i.e. the undeformed left energy. Expressing it in terms of the action of the deformed
H,P
etc. and using genericity of the undeformed CFT spectrum, one arrives at these relations
[
133
]
.
An alternate derivation
[
164
,
180
]
uses the flow equation for the Hamiltonian that follows from (
5.2
)

\mathcal{D}_{\mu}H_{T\bar{T}}=D_{T\bar{T}}=-\frac{4H_{L}H_{R}}{R+2\mu H}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}H_{J\bar{T}}=D_{J\bar{T}}=2\frac{H_{R}Q_{L}}{R-\lambda Q_{L}}

(5.7)

while
\mathcal{D}_{\mu}P=0
, where
\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}
is defined in (
5.3
). The expressions for the diagonal elements in
T\bar{T}
and
J\bar{T}
simply follow by re-expressing
\partial_{\lambda}E
in the corresponding theory in terms of the deformed energy, momentum and charge of the state. One can then easily check that the combinations (
5.5
) in
T\bar{T}
and (
5.5
) in
J\bar{T}
are covariantly constant along the flow.

As for the exact time dependence, it follows from the commutator
[H,\widetilde{L}_{m}^{\lambda}]
, as is clear from (
5.4
).
Using the fact the
\widetilde{L}_{m}
satisfy a Virasoro alegebra and the explicit expression for
\widetilde{L}_{0}
in terms of the Hamiltonian, it immediately follows that
[
180
]

[\widetilde{L}_{m}^{\lambda},H]=\alpha_{m}(H,P,Q)\widetilde{L}_{m}^{\lambda}\;\;\;\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\;\;\;\widetilde{L}_{m,S}^{\mu}(t)=e^{i\alpha_{m}(H,P,Q)t}\widetilde{L}_{m,S}^{\mu}(0)

(5.8)

The operators
\alpha_{m}
can be computed to all orders in
\hbar
.
In
T\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs

\alpha_{m}(H,P)=\frac{\sqrt{(R+2\mu H)^{2}+4\mu m\hbar(R+2\mu P)/R+4\mu^{2}m^{2}\hbar^{2}/R^{2}}-(R+2\mu H)}{2\mu}\approx\frac{m\hbar}{R}\cdot\frac{R+2\mu P}{R+2\mu H}+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{2})

(5.9)

and
\bar{\alpha}_{m}
(appearing in the commutator of the flowed right-moving generators with
H
) is given by the same expression, but with
P\rightarrow-P
. In
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs, we have

\alpha_{m}=\frac{m\hbar}{R}\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\bar{\alpha}_{m}(H_{R})=2\,\frac{R-\lambda Q_{L}-\sqrt{(R-\lambda Q_{L})^{2}-\hbar km\lambda^{2}}}{k\lambda^{2}}\approx\frac{m\hbar}{R-\lambda Q_{L}}+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{2})

(5.10)

The value of
\alpha_{m}
reflects the locality of
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs on the left. The leading term in
\bar{\alpha}_{m}
will be reproduced by the classical analysis in the next subsection. See
[
180
]
for more details, including how the expression for
\alpha_{m}
changes depending on whether it appears to the left or the right of
\widetilde{L}_{m}^{\lambda}
in (
5.8
).

The relations (
5.5
)-(
5.6
) provide a way to phrase the difference between these theories and a standard, local
2d
CFT: while they all possess a Virasoro symmetry algebra, in CFT the zero modes of the Virasoro generators coincide with the left/right Hamiltonian, whereas in
T\bar{T}
and
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs, the relation between these zero modes and the Hamiltonian is non-linear.

Finally, let us comment that one can also use the covariant derivative to flow the KdV charges in a
T\bar{T}
/
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFT, as well as other observables of interest. Similarly, one can apply the flow argument to single-trace
T\bar{T}
and
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFT, where the flow in each twisted sector can be mapped to a standard
T\bar{T}
/
J\bar{T}
flow equation on the covering space
[
186
]
, with very similar conclusions as in the double-trace case.

5.2
Classical limit of the generators and emergence of the field-dependent coordinate

The arguments above did not use the explicit form of the flow operator
\hat{\mathcal{X}}
, but only its existence. Nonetheless, to have a better handle over the symmetries, one would need explicit expressions for the flow operator and the conserved charges. As it turns out, these can be obtained without much effort in the classical limit of
T\bar{T}
and
J\bar{T}
- deformed CFTs, in which operator ordering issues can be neglected and the commutators are replaced by Poisson brackets. As we already pointed out, all Poisson brackets of the currents are known exactly in these theories, for finite deformation parameter, as follows from the fact that the deformed Hamiltonian density is a known function of the undeformed Hamiltonian, momentum and current densities, whose Poisson brackets are universal.

Concretely, in this subsection we use the explicit form of the
T\bar{T}
and
J\bar{T}
operators, which determine the Hamiltonian deformation,
to obtain an explicit expression for the corresponding flow operators in the classical limit. Then, we solve the classical counterpart of (
5.3
) for the flowed conserved currents and show how the field-dependent coordinates (
3.46
) and (
3.125
)
that have previously appeared in our discussions of these deformations

emerge
from the flow.

Classical limit of the flow operator

The flow operator,
\hat{\mathcal{X}}
is defined in (
5.2
). In the classical limit, we let
\hat{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow_{cls}i\mathcal{X}_{cls}
. For a general Smirnov-Zamolodchikov deformation,
\partial_{\lambda}H
is minus the spatial integral of the SZ operator. To find
\mathcal{X}_{cls}
, it is useful to write
\partial_{\lambda}H
as
[
212
]

\partial_{\lambda}H=-\int d\sigma\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}J^{A}_{\alpha}(\sigma)J^{B}_{\beta}(\sigma)=-\int d\sigma d\tilde{\sigma}\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}J^{A}_{\alpha}(\sigma)\delta(\sigma-\tilde{\sigma})J^{B}_{\beta}(\tilde{\sigma})

(5.11)

Introducing the Green’s function on the cylinder via
